!!! note "Research log"
    Newest entries first, though the run is not strictly sorted — some findings were written up after later ones. **Last updated: 2026-08-15 (Terraform's env-var surface enumerated from source; the docs page lists 14, the binary reads more).**

> **2026-08-15** — **The Terraform checkout has already given up its graph internals, its plan mechanics and its config schemas, so this pass took the surface nobody audits: environment variables.** Swept `os.Getenv`/`os.LookupEnv` across `internal/` and the root package, dropped the acceptance-test harness variables, and checked each survivor against the CLI environment-variables page. That page documents **14**; the binary reads at least **eight more** that are on no page at all, plus two (`TF_LOG_CORE`, `TF_LOG_PROVIDER`) documented on the debugging page instead and `TF_TOKEN_<host>` on the credentials page. Captured as [[terraform-env-vars]], with the sweep command recorded so it can be re-run on a later release. **The one with immediate practical value is `TF_DISABLE_PLUGIN_TLS`.** Terraform secures each provider's local gRPC channel with auto-generated **mutual TLS**, and any non-empty value turns that off — which makes it the way to confirm that a local traffic interceptor, not the provider, is behind `Failed to load plugin schemas` on every provider. The source is blunt that it exists for the plugin SDK test framework and **"is not intended to be set by end-users"**, so it belongs in a diagnosis, not in a shell profile; with it set the plugin channel is unauthenticated on loopback. Folded into **E5** alongside `TF_LOG`. **`TF_ENABLE_PLUGGABLE_STATE_STORAGE` closes a loop from the 2026-08-13 schema sweep**, which found a `state_store` block gated behind `AllowExperimentalFeatures` since 1.13 and announced nowhere: this is how you switch it on, introduced in `f494ff5540` and first tagged **v1.14.0**, and initialising a backend under it prints warnings that describe the feature's maturity more candidly than any published page. Recorded on [[release-feature-map]] beside the original finding. **Two more worth knowing.** `TF_WARN_OUTPUT_ERRORS` lives in a file whose only contents are a comment reading "feature flags for the next release" and downgrades **output interpolation errors to warnings**, logging at `[ERROR]` and recording the output as unknown — a pre-0.12-era compatibility shim, and deliberately *not* applied to the newer sensitive-output checks, which the source excludes by name. And **`TF_SKIP_CREATE_DEFAULT_WORKSPACE`**, new in **v1.15.0** with a matching `-create-default-workspace` flag on `init`, has inverted precedence on purpose: the variable beats an explicit `-create-default-workspace=true`, because a `true` on the command line "is indistinguishable from the default value being used". That is the first sign of Terraform treating the `default` workspace as optional, which is **A7**'s territory. Also recorded as method: variables read by **prefix scan** (`TF_VAR_`, `TF_CLI_ARGS_<name>`, `TF_TOKEN_<host>`) do not show up in the sweep and must be searched for as prefixes, and **`TF_IGNORE` is not read anywhere in the repository** — it belongs to the config-upload library, not the CLI. **A3** and **E5** both gain the pointer. No status changes.

> **2026-08-15** — **OpenTofu is the best-covered repo here, so the useful angle was the one thing the changelog-derived pages could not reach: the migration mechanism.** The feature maps and the schema sweep already answer *what OpenTofu added*; nothing answered *what it does to your project when you switch*. Captured as [[opentofu-migration-mechanics]], with three findings that appear in none of the migration documentation. **State provider addresses are translated in memory rather than rewritten on disk** — `internal/tofumigrate` deep-copies the state on every load, at `tofu init`, at every local plan/apply, and at `state show` — and, crucially, **the translation is skipped for any provider the configuration pins in full form**. `random = {}` becomes `registry.opentofu.org/hashicorp/random`; `source = "registry.terraform.io/hashicorp/random"` stays put, by design. That is the supported lever for running the OpenTofu CLI against HashiCorp-built providers, and it is stated only in a package comment. There is also an **off switch documented nowhere**: `OPENTOFU_STATEFILE_PROVIDER_ADDRESS_TRANSLATION=0`, present since **v1.6.0** (commit `9c789368dc`, [opentofu#773](https://github.com/opentofu/opentofu/pull/773)) and absent from every `.md` in the repository. **The lock-file half is the one with a real consequence.** Since **v1.10.0**, `UpgradeFromPredecessorProject` mirrors `registry.terraform.io/hashicorp/*` entries into OpenTofu-registry ones — only that namespace, because the source says no equivalence can be assumed elsewhere — preserving version selections and **discarding every hash**, since the lock "can be used to select the same version number but not to guarantee that the provider package matches what was previously installed". The warning the user sees states the underlying fact plainly: *"the OpenTofu project's provider releases are not byte-for-byte identical."* Same provider source, different builds — which is the answer to the question people actually ask about the fork's providers, and it means the switch is a **re-recording** of provider checksums rather than continuity, worth a deliberate review for anyone treating the lock as a supply-chain control (**I4**, **A6**). A migration performed on 1.6–1.9 had no lock mirroring at all. **One existing claim sharpened:** two pages described the 1.8 `.tofu` extension as "parsed alongside `.tf`". True of the directory, misleading for a pair — the parser "will ignore the `.tf` file and only use the `.tofu` file", so `main.tofu` must be a **complete replacement** for `main.tf`, not a patch. Corrected in E3 and [[opentofu-feature-history]]. The same shadowing covers `.tf.json`/`.tofu.json` and the test extensions, which surfaced something the notes had never recorded: OpenTofu's test framework accepts **`.tofutest.hcl`** and `.tofutest.json`. **E3 stays ⬜.**

> **2026-08-15** — **Reading `hashicorp/hcl` overturned an assumption the notes had already reasoned from: the two tools do not build against the same HCL.** Checked in the two `go.mod` files rather than assumed — **Terraform pins upstream `github.com/hashicorp/hcl/v2 v2.24.0`**, while **OpenTofu requires `v2.20.1` and `replace`s it with its own fork**, `github.com/opentofu/hcl/v2 v2.20.2-0.20251021132045-587d123c2828`, a pseudo-version from a commit dated 2025-10-21. Different code, from bases four minor versions apart. [[conditional-branch-evaluation]] had written "it holds for OpenTofu identically — both consume the same `hcl/v2`", which is a false premise; **the conclusion survives** because it rests on `hclsyntax/spec.md`, which both implement, but the reasoning did not, and it is corrected in place with the general rule: **spec-level claims transfer between the tools, implementation-level ones need checking per tool.** Captured as [[hcl-library-facts]], with the upstream delta enumerated from the CHANGELOG and gated with `git tag --contains` — mark propagation through conditional and `for` expressions (**v2.23.0**, the machinery deciding whether `sensitive` survives them) and rejection of invalid nested splats (**v2.24.0**) are the two that would be visible in configuration terms. Recorded with an explicit limit: OpenTofu's fork carries ~19 months of its own commits and may have back-ported any of them, so the table is **an upper bound on divergence, not a measurement** — the fork is not cloned locally. **Spec facts captured for the book, quoted rather than paraphrased:** identifiers are `UAX #31` plus a dash that the spec itself calls unidiomatic; numeric literals are decimal only, with no hex or octal syntax in the language; **escape sequences are defined only for *quoted* templates**, which independently confirms Ch 7's existing claim that a backslash in a heredoc is literal; `<<-` strips "the minimum number of leading spaces", not all indentation, and only from lines starting with a literal string; and strip markers work at **syntax level**, so `${"hello" ~}${" world"}` still produces `"hello world"`. **One gap found in a written chapter, additive rather than contradictory, so nothing flips to 🔄:** Ch 7 teaches `[*]` correctly but never covers the **attribute-only `.*.` form**, whose trailing index applies to the result list where `[*]`'s applies inside each element, nor the splat rules that have no `for` equivalent — a non-collection auto-wraps (`any_number.*` is `[any_number]`) and a **null scalar splats to an empty tuple**, which is the idiomatic zero-or-one conversion. Logged for the next revision of that chapter. **Also worth knowing for future lookups:** `dynamic` blocks and `try`/`can` live in `ext/`, so they are HCL *extensions* rather than language features, which is why the specs never mention them — and HCL defines no functions of its own, the host application supplies the table, which is how Terraform's and OpenTofu's function lists can differ without either breaking the spec. **B4** gains the specs as a reference for when the docs paraphrase; **E3** gains the fork finding. No status changes.

> **2026-08-15** — **KL is cited in nine places, so this pass was a citation audit, and two citations pointed at content the repository does not contain.** Both were checked by grepping the whole checkout rather than reading lab titles. **I3 cited Lab 06 as a "dynamic-blocks section": there are zero `dynamic "…"` blocks in the entire repo.** The lab's title, "Making Code Dynamic and Reusable", uses *dynamic* in the plain-English sense — its steps are variables, data sources and string interpolation, and its own wrap-up says "to see how dynamic our code is now, update the value of the `environment` variable". **I5 cited the same lab as a "reusable-module section": there are zero `module "…"` blocks anywhere, and the word *module* does not appear in that lab at all.** "Reusable" there means parameterized. Both slots now carry a ⚠️ saying plainly that KL does not cover the topic, which is more useful than a silently wrong link. Inventory and evidence in [[krausen-labs-facts]]. **A third, smaller mismatch:** lab 03's *directory* is `lab_03_working_with_variables_and_dependencies` while the lab is titled "Working with Variables and **Outputs**" and teaches variables, `tfvars`, outputs and precedence — dependencies are lab 04's subject. Both link labels relabelled, since the directory name is what the path had copied. **What KL actually is:** eight labs, each written three times for **AWS, Azure and GitHub**, with 01–05 cumulative and 06–08 standalone; 01–05 are instructions only, 06–08 ship starter `.tf`. Lab 08 is the pick of them — it keeps the `count` version of the resources beside the `for_each` version so the address difference is visible rather than asserted, which is exactly **I1**'s lesson. **Coverage measured by grep, and it stops early:** `count` appears in 25 files, `terraform state` in 4, `for_each` in 3, `depends_on` in 2 — while `backend "…"`, `terraform workspace`, `import {}`, `lifecycle`, `provisioner`, `terraform test`, `taint` and `terraform graph` appear in **none**. So KL is a strong companion for B1–B8 and I1 and nothing past it, which matters because the 004 exam does test backends and state management. **Two README claims that do not hold:** it promises a pre-configured dev container and tells you to create a Codespace "from this repo", but there is **no `.devcontainer`** — the only hidden directory is `.github` holding `FUNDING.yml`, and the Codespaces badge points at a *separate* `btkrausen/terraform-codespaces` repository; and its "Terraform 1.12.2 — Tested" badge sits beside a CI badge pointing at `terraform-testing`, which installs Latest, validates a **different** set of lab directories, and per [[krausen-lab-ci-facts]] has a plan gate that cannot fail. The B3 lab entry now carries the Codespaces caveat. No status changes.

> **2026-08-15** — **`btkrausen/terraform-testing` turned out not to be a testing-library repo at all, and is more useful than if it had been: it is a GitHub Actions harness, and it is a ready-made A3 case study — good pattern and cautionary tale in one file.** Captured as [[krausen-lab-ci-facts]]. Three workflows run a matrix over 16 lab directories per provider, on push, on PR, and on a **weekly cron** — and that cron is the transferable idea, since its purpose is not to test the author's code but to detect the day *someone else's* release breaks it. The pattern half is worth copying wholesale and is now step 4 of A3: **OIDC rather than static keys**, the third-party action **pinned by commit SHA**, an explicit least-privilege `permissions:` block, `TF_IN_AUTOMATION` plus `TF_CLI_ARGS: -no-color`, `terraform_wrapper: false` so step outputs are real exit codes, **`init -backend=false`** so validation never touches real state, and `fail-fast: false` with per-leg `mktemp -d` isolation. **The cautionary half is a matched pair of exit-code bugs in the same repository, failing in opposite directions**, which is why they earned a callout rather than a footnote. The workflow runs `terraform plan -detailed-exitcode || true` and then `echo $? > plan_exit_code.txt` — `$?` is the status of `true`, so the file always holds `0`, the checking step always reports success, and **the plan gate cannot fail**; a lab whose plan errors keeps the badge green. Present in all four jobs across all three workflows. The repo's standalone script captures the code correctly with `PLAN_EXIT_CODE=$?` but opens with `set -e`, under which the value `2` — "plan succeeded, changes present", the normal outcome — aborts the script before that line, so **the branch written to translate 2 into success is unreachable** and the gate cannot pass. `-detailed-exitcode` is genuinely awkward to consume: the value meaning "fine" is the value shell error handling treats as failure. This is the same lesson the path already draws about `terraform fmt -check`, with better evidence. **Two more, both silent:** the failure-notification job is gated on `needs.test-labs.result` while the jobs are named `test-tfb-labs` and `test-hol-labs`, so it never runs and the README's promise of an auto-filed issue does not happen — a skipped job looks like a passing one in a run summary, so alerting needs one deliberate failure to prove it — and the README says each module is run through `fmt`, which appears nowhere in the workflows. **Scope note recorded so the labs are not mis-trusted:** **KL** (`terraform-associate-labs`) is a different repository from the `modules/tfb/**` labs this harness checks, so nothing here changes KL's standing; what it changes is what the green badge supports — `init` and `validate` failures are caught for real, `plan` failures are not caught at all. Also noted: the AWS workflow's two jobs use two different credential models, OIDC in one and static access keys in the other, which is a partial migration frozen in place. **A3 stays ⬜.**

> **2026-08-15** — **The last unmined local repo is a course, not a tool, so the question it answers is different: what does citing TF2026 actually buy?** Read all thirteen lecture directories of `devopsforge2304/Terraform-Full-Course-2026` (`4f1928e`, 2026-03-25) and mapped each to a topic in [[tf2026-course-facts]]. The path cited three timestamps and never mentioned the companion repo at all; the resources table now carries it. **The finding that earned a callout is a direct conflict with A7.** Lecture 09 teaches environment management as CLI workspaces, with `lookup(var.instance_type, terraform.workspace)` over a `{ dev, staging, prod }` map — production separated from development by one string inside one backend, one credential set and one directory, which is exactly what A7's reference #2 exists to contradict. A7 already carries a ⚠️ for an *official tutorial* doing the same thing, so this is the second source teaching it; the warning now names both. The demo also has a real bug worth quoting, since it is the kind a learner hits before they can even see the lesson: a two-argument `lookup` **errors on a missing key**, and the map has no `default` entry, so the configuration fails in the `default` workspace — the one you are in before running `terraform workspace new`. **Three more aged idioms, each checked against current docs rather than assumed.** The backend lecture locks with `dynamodb_table`, which HashiCorp's S3 backend reference now deprecates in favour of `use_lockfile`, saying DynamoDB locking "will be removed in a future minor version". The debugging lecture recommends `terraform refresh`, deprecated since **v0.15.4** in favour of `-refresh-only` on `plan`/`apply`. And its import story is the CLI `terraform import` only — the declarative **`import` block** (1.5+) and `moved`/`removed` appear nowhere in the repository. **The gap that decides placement:** the README's section 10 is called "Terraform Security, Testing and Validation" and its testing table is `fmt`, `validate`, `tflint`, `checkov` — there is **no `terraform test`, no `.tftest.hcl`, no `precondition`/`postcondition`/`check` anywhere**, so the course cannot serve **A2** and the path is right not to cite it there. Its secrets material is sound, and matches **A6** on the point that matters: `sensitive = true` hides a value from CLI output while it stays in state, so the feature "alone is not sufficient for production security". **Also recorded:** the configurations are not runnable as written, since they hardcode one account's AMI, subnet, security-group and key-pair IDs plus globally unique bucket names, and the repo contains **no OpenTofu material at all** — the strings `tofu` and `opentofu` return zero hits. Nothing in it reaches the Advanced or Expert half. Verdict kept in the note so the course is not re-evaluated topic by topic: cite it for B1–B8 and the mechanical parts of Intermediate, not for testing, environment isolation, or state surgery. One unrelated tidy while in the file: **E5's reference list had two items numbered 5**. No status changes.

> **2026-08-15** — **Floci was the one local repo already written up, so this pass was a re-verification, and it moved three claims.** The checkout is a **fork**: `origin` is a personal one and **`upstream` is `floci-io/floci`**, with the working tree parked on the `1.6.0` release commit (2026-08-06) while `upstream/main` runs to 2026-08-15 with **no tag since** — so released and unreleased behaviour needed labelling separately, and [[floci-facts]] now says which is which. **The claim that changed shape:** "repeat `CreateBucket` in `us-east-1` is idempotent here, AWS is not" is narrower than recorded. `S3Service.createBucket` *does* raise `BucketAlreadyOwnedByYou` (409), guarded by a region check on both sides, so the plain repeat-create case matches AWS deliberately. What still diverges is the **creation-tags** case, where the controller re-runs `putBucketTagging` on the existing bucket — a repeat create silently *rewrites* the tags instead of failing. The second open item, missing `InvalidTag` validation, is unchanged: the string appears nowhere in the source. One item found on the good side — an explicit `<LocationConstraint>us-east-1</LocationConstraint>` is rejected with `InvalidLocationConstraint`, which is AWS's behaviour. **The authentication caveat is now confirmed in code and is starting to change.** `S3RequestAuthorizationParser` parses a signed request only far enough to pull the **access key ID** out of the `Authorization` header, so identity yes, signature no — which is exactly what the empirical test found. But a **per-service** `services.s3.enforce-auth` switch exists now, separate from the global `FLOCI_AUTH_VALIDATE_SIGNATURES`, and commit `3fe836aa` (2026-08-15) added genuine SigV4 verification **for pre-signed URLs** under it, with a test asserting a tampered signature is rejected. Unreleased, pre-signed only, and the flag itself is undocumented — so the undocumented-flag pattern moved rather than went away, while `FLOCI_SERVICES_IAM_ENFORCEMENT_ENABLED`, which the note had called undocumented, **is documented now**. **The find that changes how much to trust the labs:** Floci ships `compatibility-tests/` with `compat-terraform`, `compat-opentofu` and `compat-cdk` beside five SDK suites, running real clients against a live instance with no mocks — and the Terraform case drives an **S3 remote backend with a DynamoDB lock table**, so **I6**'s backend work is a path upstream tests. With one trap recorded: their `backend.hcl` uses `endpoint`, `dynamodb_endpoint`, `force_path_style` and `dynamodb_table`, all of which HashiCorp's current S3 backend reference marks deprecated in favour of an `endpoints {}` block, `use_path_style` and `use_lockfile` (DynamoDB locking "will be removed in a future minor version"), so a lab must not copy it verbatim. The **B2** emulator callout gains the compatibility-suite and IAM-enforcement facts, and its bucket-tag example is now past tense with the 1.6.0 fix named. Also recorded: `FLOCI_SERVICES_IAM_SEED_DEPLOYER_PRINCIPAL` for a real lab principal instead of the god-mode `test` key, and `FLOCI_PROTOCOLS_REJECT_UNKNOWN_SERVICE_SCOPE`, which is why an unimplemented service returns a clean error rather than a **misleading `NoSuchBucket`**. No status changes.

> **2026-08-15** — **Reading Terrakube's source rather than its docs overturned the A9 claim that mattered most: its permissions are no longer organization-wide.** The old callout said toggles apply across a whole organization with no project tier, so a tenant had to be an entire Terrakube organization — true of the pre-2.30 model, and wrong now. **RBAC v2** landed in **2.30.0** (2026-02-26, commit `5cfc9638`) with HCP-shaped roles (`admin`, `write`, `plan`, `read`, `custom`), and **project-level team permissions** in **2.32.0** (2026-05-17, commit `e99e40c9`), both gated by `git tag --contains`. Permissions now hang off three entities — `Team`, `ProjectAccess`, `Access` — so **a tenant can be a project**, which removes the main structural objection A9 raised against it. The verdict paragraph, which called the open-source permission surface uniformly coarse, now names Terrakube as the exception and keeps the charge against Atlantis. Captured as [[terrakube-facts]]. **Why the source was necessary:** unlike the other two checkouts, Terrakube's docs are a separate site, and the model is stated loosely there and precisely in the entity classes, which is a consequence of the API being built on **Elide** — authorization lives in annotations on the fields, so `Team.java` *is* the permission spec. Two behaviours only readable there: under a predefined role the fine-grained booleans are **ignored**, and a null or blank role normalizes to `custom`, which is what silently preserves pre-2.30 rows. The roles are also not a ladder — `write` plans and applies, but registry, VCS and template management are `admin`-only. **Three capabilities were missing from the path entirely.** `RemoteTfeController` implements the TFE v2 API at `/remote/tfe/v2/`, including workspace lock/unlock, state and configuration versions, run apply/discard, and `remote-state-consumers` — so the **`cloud {}` block works against a self-hosted Terrakube** and cross-workspace state reads are a modelled relationship rather than a bucket IAM condition, which was the specific gap A9 left open for the pure-CI design. There is an **HCP Terraform importer** carrying workspaces, variables, variable sets, tags and state versions, with a preview step for sensitive ones. And **federated credentials** (2.31.0) invert the dynamic-credentials pattern: a CI job authenticates *to* the platform with a workload-identity token matched on issuer, audience and claims, list-valued claims matching any element, which is exactly a GitHub Actions token's shape. **The run-isolation finding deserved its own callout**, since it is the constraint the whole topic turns on: the ephemeral executor runs **each job as its own Kubernetes Job**, and `EPHEMERAL_CONFIG_POD_ANNOTATIONS` annotates the generated pod so Vault Agent Injector or GKE Workload Identity can give that single run its own credentials. Recorded with its limit — the tenant's code still runs inside that pod, so plan-time code execution still reaches whatever identity the pod holds. Status unchanged: **A9** stays ⬜ — the topic gained accuracy, not completion.

> **2026-08-15** — **Terratest was three docs links and one wrong version fact; it is now source-derived, and the fact was wrong in a way that mattered.** [[terraform-testing]] recorded "Terratest latest published 2026-05-11," which is really **v1.0.0**, the first stable release — v1.0.1 followed 2026-06-27 and the v2 betas on 2026-07-20 and 2026-08-10. Corrected there and captured properly as [[terratest-facts]]. **A wrinkle in the checkout worth recording as method:** the working tree is a detached HEAD at v1.0.1-era code, thirty commits behind `origin/main`, and `main` is where the **v2 restructure** lives — so `ls modules/` locally contradicts the current package list. Everything v2 here was read with `git show origin/main:<path>`, which needs no checkout change and leaves the user's tree alone. **v2 is a multi-module split, not a rewrite:** sixteen independently versioned Go modules under `/v2` paths, so a Terraform-only test stops pulling in every cloud SDK; `random`/`retry`/`logger`/`shell`/`files` consolidate into `modules/core`; `http-helper`, `test-structure` and `dns-helper` lose their hyphens; and six packages — `collections`, `environment`, `git`, `version-checker`, `slack`, `oci` — were **deleted outright** in one commit, so those imports do not port by editing a path. The `go.work` file's own comment explains the release discipline, since tags are cut with `GOWORK=off` and a script enforces it. **The teachable API fact came from diffing tags, not from docs:** `modules/terraform/apply.go` at `v1.0.1` carries both `Apply` and `ApplyContext` with `// Deprecated:` on the bare form, and on `origin/main` only the `*Context` variants survive — so the four-variant convention (`Foo` / `FooE` / `FooContext` / `FooContextE`) is worth teaching as the thing that makes the whole API readable, and writing `*Context` calls today *is* the v2 migration. **Three hygiene rules got their own callout** because each one silently breaks a suite: `defer` the destroy or a failed assertion leaks paid resources; `-count=1` or Go's test cache returns a stale pass when only `.tf` files changed; `-timeout 30m` or Go kills the process at ten minutes and the deferred destroy never runs. Added `random.UniqueID()` namespacing and `ApplyAndIdempotentContext` to the A2 milestone. **One tie-in to the previous entry:** Terratest ships a full `terragrunt` helper module — `run --all`, `stack generate`, `stack run`, `stack output`, `dag graph` — so **E4**'s stack layout is testable end to end, noted there. Status unchanged: **A2** stays ⬜.

> **2026-08-15** — **Terragrunt was a name-drop in four places and a link to a docs root; it is now a source-derived reference, mined from the local checkout rather than the web.** The clone at `repos\terragrunt` (commit `54c43a44c`, tags to **v1.1.3**) ships its own documentation site under `docs/`, which is what renders at docs.terragrunt.com — so the checkout gives the docs, a per-release changelog as one file per change, and the code to settle anything the docs leave vague, all version-gated by tag. Captured as [[terragrunt-facts]]. **The framing correction is the biggest finding:** the path introduced Terragrunt as the DRY tool, and Terragrunt's own terminology page says that focus "has shifted to offering more tooling for *orchestrating* infrastructure," warning you "might miss the forest for the trees" by evaluating it on DRY alone. E4 and A7 now present it as a run orchestrator — discovery, run queue, runner pool, cross-unit DAG — with the vocabulary the docs use precisely, including the adage that **your current working directory is your blast radius**. **Three capabilities had no representation at all in the path:** `--filter`, a query language selecting units by name, path glob, config attribute, graph traversal or **Git diff**, which supersedes the whole `--queue-*-dir` flag pile and is the honest answer to "apply only what my PR changed" (dry-run it on `find`, reuse it verbatim on `run --all`); **explicit stacks**, a `terragrunt.stack.hcl` blueprint generating units into a gitignored `.terragrunt-stack/`; and the **content-addressable store**, on by default since 1.1.0, where each getter resolves through a cheap probe (`git ls-remote`, an HTTP `ETag`, `HeadObject`, an OCI manifest digest) and hard-links deduplicated blobs, which is why large estates stay fast for reasons beyond parallelism. **The CLI migration is worse than the `run-all` → `run --all` rename the path recorded:** the per-command `*-all` forms were removed outright rather than deprecated, env vars are `TG_`-prefixed, and several flag renames are not mechanical — `--terragrunt-debug` is `--inputs-debug`, and `--terragrunt-fail-on-state-bucket-creation` has no successor because backend provisioning became its own `backend bootstrap` command. **A naming collision now has a warning in E2:** a Terraform Stack (`.tfstack.hcl`, HCP-run) and a Terragrunt stack (`terragrunt.stack.hcl`, open CLI) share a word and nothing else. Two in-repo doc pages are themselves stale — one still calls Terragrunt pre-1.0, another still calls `terragrunt.stack.hcl` unshipped — so the note says to read the reference pages, not the narrative ones. Status unchanged: **E4** stays ⬜; this is reading material for it, not the topic done.

> **2026-08-15** — **The open-source half went from an architecture sketch to a buildable blueprint, and it answers the "is this doable" question with a yes.** The previous pass named where each control relocates but stopped before the mechanism, and "use CI OIDC" is not an architecture until the claim is named. Grounded it on GitHub's OIDC docs: the subject is `repo:octo-org/octo-repo:environment:prod`, conditioned in the cloud trust policy exactly as HCP's `organization:…:project:…:workspace:…` is, with the same wildcard failure mode, and the token is "only valid for a single job, and then automatically expires", which is what makes the shared-runner risk survivable. **Two timely findings.** Repositories "created after July 15, 2026 use an immutable default subject format that includes owner and repository IDs" rather than names, not available on GitHub Enterprise Server — that is a direct fix for the reuse hazard this topic flagged in offboarding, since an ID never repeats and a stale trust policy cannot be reactivated by recreating a same-named thing; trust policies written against the old format need re-checking. And **repository custom properties can be surfaced as OIDC claims**, enabling ABAC "driven by repository metadata rather than hard-coded allow lists", which is the open-source counterpart to HCP's project segment and the thing that makes fifty tenants tractable. Also wrote the state recipe (prefix per tenant, IAM condition, per-tenant KMS key, OpenTofu encryption on top), and the pipeline-ownership mechanism (CODEOWNERS on the workflow path, or a platform-owned reusable workflow), which is the structural equivalent of `Manage policies` being organization-level. **The finding worth carrying: open source is strictly stronger on modules.** HCP's private registry has no per-team read scope, so tenants who must not see each other's modules need separate organizations; with git sources, module visibility *is* repository visibility, so the capability HCP charges an organization split for is the VCS's default. Verdict recorded in the topic: achievable, stronger on module visibility and state encryption, harder on run isolation, coarse permission surfaces, and the absence of a single console answering "who can reach this tenant".

> **2026-08-15** — **A9 was an HCP topic wearing a general title; it now covers the open-source stack too.** The old treatment was a single callout saying OpenTofu has no user model and naming three products. Replaced with real coverage, and the framing that makes it teachable is that **the four controls relocate rather than disappear**: who may start a run becomes VCS branch protection, what the run may touch becomes CI OIDC federation, policy becomes a pipeline step the tenant cannot edit, and state access becomes backend IAM on bucket, prefix and key. Two properties transfer badly and are called out — there is no equivalent of Remote State Sharing, so cross-workspace reads must be an object-prefix IAM condition, and the audit trail becomes the CI's plus the cloud's, usually better than HCP's 14 days but requiring manual correlation. **The load-bearing finding came from Atlantis's own security page**, and it governs every self-hosted design: `apply` approval is not a boundary, because *"it is possible to run malicious code in a `terraform plan` using the external data source or by specifying a malicious provider"*, which "could then exfiltrate your credentials". A single runner holding every tenant's credentials therefore has no boundary regardless of approval rules, so the rule is one runner per trust domain with per-run OIDC credentials. Atlantis details recorded: authorization runs **twice** (repo, then per project after pre-workflow hooks, since affected projects are unknown at the first check), `--gh-team-allowlist` maps VCS teams to commands with the documented trap that omitting `policy_check` makes manual plans skip policy while autoplans still run it, and the **external command** in server-side config is the real per-tenant hook. **Terrakube** (Apache-2.0) is the closest workalike but scopes differently: teams are named after **Dex** groups so membership federates by construction, while permissions are **organization-wide booleans**, so a tenant is an organization rather than a project — though its separate **Manage State** toggle is better-grained than HCP, where all four fixed workspace roles read full state. **Digger** (MIT) sidesteps the runner problem by executing in the user's existing CI. **A rename worth catching:** `terrateamio/terrateam` now redirects to `stategraph/stategraph` (MPL-2.0, OCaml) — Terrateam's engine was open-sourced and the product is folding into Stategraph, which stores state as a graph in PostgreSQL; **A4**'s TID §8.7 citation now carries that warning, since the book's six-way comparison predates the move. Closed with **OpenTofu state encryption as the one tenant control HCP lacks**, per-tenant KMS keys making a bucket-wide read useless, alongside its documented limit that encryption "cannot protect the sensitive values in the state file from the person running the `tofu` command" — it defends a tenant against the operator and other tenants, never against itself. Plus a choosing-between-them callout, since the real decision is where the boundary lives rather than which vendor.

> **2026-08-15** — **The three "owned by another topic" areas now carry their multi-tenant halves, in both places.** A9 had deferred self-service, policy authoring and Stacks to E6, A5 and E2, which was right about ownership and wrong about completeness, since each behaves differently than its own topic implies once tenants exist. Each now has a multi-tenant note where it lives, plus a summarising callout in A9. **E6** turned out to hold the most useful finding: no-code provisioning is the one platform capability a tenant can run for itself, because it needs `Manage all projects`, `Manage all workspaces` **or project Admin**, and custom project permissions need only create-workspaces, write-variables and apply-runs. The mechanics fit the boundary exactly — provisioning picks a project, "teams with access to the specified project can view the workspace automatically", and "if the specified project contains any project-scoped variable sets, HCP Terraform automatically applies those sets", which is A9's per-tenant credential path arriving with no manual step. Two cautions recorded: **auto apply is the default** for no-code workspaces, and no-code readiness is a registry property, so the registry's missing read scope means every tenant can provision every no-code module. **A5** splits cleanly in two: policy *sets* scope to projects, workspaces or tags, but *authoring* needs organization-level `Manage policies`, which "implicitly gives permission to read runs on all workspaces", so no tenant can write its own policy without org-wide run visibility; the workable division is tenants choosing sets and the platform team owning the code. **E2** was the pleasant surprise — Stacks close the hole A9 spent a correction on, since the `store` block binds a variable set by name or ID and "variable precedence does not affect Stacks", so the workspace-variable credential override is impossible there. The cost is availability rather than confidentiality: removing a referenced variable set makes the Stack fail to deploy.

> **2026-08-15** — **Third coverage check on A9: onboarding was complete, the lifecycle was not.** Every control in the topic described standing a tenant up, and nothing described taking one down, which is where the sharpest docs sentence in this whole area lives: **"Terraform does not automatically destroy managed infrastructure when you delete a workspace."** Delete workspace and state, and the tenant's resources keep running unmanaged. The ordering is forced from below, since workspace deletion is guarded by default to unlocked workspaces managing no infrastructure (organization owners can **force delete** past it, or delegate that), while "you can only delete projects that do not contain Stacks or workspaces". Wrote the full teardown, because each step maps to something the topic had introduced separately and each is a leak if skipped: revoke team tokens, delete the team before a directory assertion repopulates it, unscope variable sets, policy sets and agent pools (recalling that unscoping a pool does not detach active users), remove the project from other workspaces' remote-state-sharing lists, and delete the cloud role, since **no HCP action touches a trust policy** and an orphaned one naming a deleted project goes live again if that project name is reused. Two edition facts folded in: ephemeral workspaces with project-inherited auto-destroy (Standard/Premium, and their runs skip apply approval), and **data retention policies being Terraform Enterprise only**, so "how long does a departed tenant's state persist" has a different answer per product. Second gap closed: an explicit **organization-versus-project decision rule**, built from the three things the topic found to be organization-wide and indivisible — registry visibility, the cost-estimation toggle, and the org permissions that widen read as a side effect — with the trade recorded that separate organizations lose cross-org registry sharing on HCP, which only Terraform Enterprise offers. Third: the **platform workspace** that holds the `tfe` provider token is the highest-privilege object in the estate and now says so, since the milestone asks for the access model as code and that workspace's state can rewrite every tenant's permissions.

> **2026-08-15** — **A9's five stated omissions closed, so the topic no longer defers anything.** The previous pass listed SSO mapping, audit trails, token attribution, non-AWS credentials and chargeback as deliberate exclusions; all five are now written from verbatim docs, and the exclusions callout is gone. **SAML mapping** turns out to be the one with teeth: membership is authoritative and re-evaluated at every login, "this automatic mapping overrides any manually set team memberships", so a directory edit moves a person between tenants and a manual UI fix survives only until their next sign-in. Two failure modes recorded, one silent and one fail-open — a team name not matching exactly is ignored with no team created, and a **missing attribute** drops users into a default `sso` team while "not removing them from any existing teams", so a broken assertion preserves stale access instead of revoking it. Also the permanent owner bypass: non-owners must use SSO, owners keep username-and-password access by design, hence the two break-glass accounts. **Audit trails** carry three constraints that shape any design: 14-day retention, Standard/Premium only and **not available in Terraform Enterprise**, and an endpoint that "cannot be accessed with a user token or team token", requiring an organization or audit-trail token, which is why the exporter is structurally a platform-team job. **Team tokens** trade attribution for durability, and the docs state the worst case plainly, that an owners-team token "will have all of the same permissions that an organization owner would" — one string crossing every boundary in the topic. **The per-cloud comparison** was the most useful find: the `sub` claim is identical everywhere but the matching primitive is not. AWS conditions on `StringEquals`/`StringLike`, GCP uses a workload identity pool attribute condition with `assertion.sub.startsWith(...)` so a project prefix is natural, and **Azure cannot wildcard at all** — the federated credential's subject "is a direct string match", requiring "two federated identity credentials … for each workspace", one per run phase. So Azure is safe by construction and heavy to automate, while AWS is flexible and one wildcard from being wrong. **Chargeback** closes on a constraint rather than a feature: cost estimation is a single organization-wide toggle with no per-project granularity, and gating on it forces the policy set onto **Legacy** policy execution mode capped at Sentinel 0.40.x, which is the strongest argument in the topic for doing cost governance with Infracost in the pipeline instead.

> **2026-08-15** — **Coverage audit of A9 found two controls missing entirely.** Asked whether the topic was complete, checked instead of answering, and it was not. **Remote State Sharing** was absent, which is a real hole under the state-boundary heading, because team permissions answer which *people* may read a state and this setting answers which *workspaces* may read it during a run. Three modes (organization, project, named list), defaulting to no sharing, with the project mode being the tenant-shaped one. It also carries a documented bypass worth knowing before trusting an audit: "Remote state access controls do not apply when using the `tfe_outputs` data source", so that path is governed by token permissions alone. Local execution mode moves the check to a user token as well. And a reviewer with scoped permissions cannot see the full list of workspaces that can reach a state, so the audit itself has a visibility floor. Second gap: **agent pools**, the compute half of the boundary, which scope to workspaces, projects or Stacks and then vanish from other workspaces' lists. Two catches recorded: `Manage agent pools` "implicitly grants access to read all workspaces and projects", making it the second org-level permission after `Manage policy overrides` that widens read access as a side effect; and scoping is not revocation, since it "does not remove it from workspaces, projects, or Stacks that are actively using it". Also added an explicit statement of what the topic stops short of — SSO/SAML team mapping, audit trails, team-token attribution, the non-AWS credential mechanics, and chargeback — so the boundary of the topic is stated rather than implied.

> **2026-08-15** — **Review of the A9 extension caught a claimed control that does not exist.** The extension asserted that putting per-tenant credential variables in a project-owned variable set stops a tenant repointing them, because project-owned sets need project Write or above. Checked against the variables pages, and it is wrong twice. **"Workspace-specific variables always overwrite variables from variable sets that have the same key"**, and the docs use credential swapping as the motivating example, so workspace **Write** alone is enough to repoint `TFC_AWS_RUN_ROLE_ARN`; the `OVERWRITTEN` badge is a notification, not a control. The actual control is marking the set **priority**, which inverts precedence so the set beats more specific scopes. The topic now says so, and says the thing that survives: repointing gains nothing anyway because the minted token still carries the attacker's own project in its `sub` claim, so **the trust policy is the control that holds and the variable placement is hygiene**. A design leaning on variable placement is one workspace variable from failing. Also found a **documentation conflict** worth recording rather than resolving: the project permission table marks `Manage variable sets` Admin-only, while the variables page grants project-owned variable set management to Write, Maintain, Admin, a `Manage variable sets` permission, or organization `Manage all projects`. Flagged in the topic with advice to verify per organization. Third fix: the registry callout said every organization registry permission is a publish permission, which overstated it, since two of the five govern attaching project tags and reserved keys; the point stands that none of them is a read scope.

> **2026-08-15** — **A9 extended from the permission tour into the four controls that actually make a tenant boundary.** The first draft covered HCP's access model well and gestured at everything else with cross-links. Four areas are now written properly, each from verbatim docs. **Per-tenant credentials:** the OIDC workload identity token's `sub` claim is `organization:ORG:project:PROJECT:workspace:WORKSPACE:run_phase:PHASE`, so the tenant boundary is expressible as a `StringEquals` condition in the AWS trust policy, which is the one place a tenant cannot edit its way around — the role ARN need not be secret. The hazard is the docs' own convenience variant, which relaxes to `StringLike` with `run_phase:*`; the same wildcard one segment left silently widens the role to the whole organization. HashiCorp's stated floor is checking audience and organization; the path records that as the floor and the *project* as the tenant boundary. Also folded in: split `TFC_AWS_PLAN_ROLE_ARN` / `TFC_AWS_APPLY_ROLE_ARN` roles as the credential-side analogue of the workspace Plan role, and **project-owned variable sets** as the delivery mechanism, since they need project Write or above, so a tenant cannot repoint its own credentials. **Cross-account:** the AWS provider's `assume_role` block, its `policy`/`policy_arns` session restrictions, and role chaining by repeating the block. Went to the AWS confused-deputy page rather than asserting from memory, and it corrected the draft: `external_id` is not "a secret the platform knows" — AWS's rule is that the value "must be unique among Example Corp's customers and controlled by Example Corp, not its customers", so a platform that lets tenants pick their own external ID has the field and none of the protection. **State as the boundary:** the four state levels read as three consumers rather than a ladder, with `Read outputs only` as the cross-tenant level that `terraform_remote_state` needs, and a CLI-versus-API asymmetry worth knowing, since the docs require read-and-write for the `terraform state` subcommands while plain Read already returns complete state files through the API. **Governance:** policy sets scope to projects, workspaces or tags (tags beta) and support exclusions, but the private registry has **no per-team read scope at all** — private modules are "only available to members of that organization", every registry permission in the org table is a publish permission, so tenants that must not see each other's modules need separate organizations, and consumption is controllable only through Sentinel. Two override traps recorded: organization **Manage policy overrides** "also gives teams read access to all workspaces in the organization" (use Delegate instead), and a policy set's own override switch beats even `Hard mandatory`. Closes with blast radius across tenants being an **identity** property where A7's is a **graph and state** property, and the four questions the four controls answer.

> **2026-08-15** — **New topic: A9 — Multitenancy, teams & RBAC.** The path covered environment isolation (**A7**), policy (**A5**), secrets (**A6**) and self-service (**E6**), but nothing about serving mutually distrusting *people* from one platform. A7 even quoted the docs line that workspaces "are not appropriate for … deployments requiring separate credentials and access controls" and then dropped the second half of it; A9 is that half, and A7 now points at it. Built from the four HCP permission pages plus the Teams overview, fetched verbatim with `fetch_page.py` rather than summarized, which was the right call: **the finding that anchors the topic only exists in the role table.** `Read state` is ✅ for **all four** fixed workspace roles, Read included, so the least-privileged preset still exposes every secret the configuration touched, and only a **custom role** can narrow it to `Read outputs only`. Project roles repeat the pattern. Four more facts folded in as callouts: permissions are **additive with the most permissive winning**, so an organization-level grant silently overrides a scoped workspace role while the reverse does not hold; the boundary leaks through **VCS merge rights, API bots and run tasks** (10-minute tokens), with HashiCorp's own line that "an integrated system is able to delegate any level of access that it has been granted"; **Sentinel mocks** are a state read by another name, downloadable at Write and "may contain unredacted sensitive information"; and **secret teams** are invisible to workspace and project admins who are not organization owners, so an access audit can be incomplete by design. Book coverage checked before citing: neither book covers HCP RBAC, so A9 cites **TUR Ch 10 §"A Workflow for Deploying Infrastructure Code" (pp. 390–411)** for the pre-HCP form (live repo vs modules repo, and the five mitigations for a CI server that must hold admin credentials), extracting the chapter to `pdf-cache` to verify rather than trusting the TOC, and carries an explicit no-book-covers-this callout. TID Ch 10 "Advanced Terraform topics" was checked and rejected — it is naming, networking, provisioners, external/local providers and checks, with nothing on access control. OpenTofu divergence recorded as an absence: the CLI has no user model, so the RBAC plane is Terrakube (Apache-2.0, verified via the GitHub API), Atlantis, or one of the commercial platforms TID §8.7 compares. Advanced re-estimated **60 → 65 hrs**; checkpoint gains a tenant-isolation bullet.

> **2026-08-15** — **Observability with OpenTelemetry researched across all three layers, and the path's own claim about it was false.** E5 said "Terraform has no built-in OTel equivalent". It has one: `OTEL_TRACES_EXPORTER=otlp` enables an OTLP exporter in `telemetry.go`, and the reason nobody knows is that **it is undocumented** — a grep of the Terraform repo's `website/` for `opentelemetry` returns nothing, so the only public description is a source comment reading "BEWARE! This is not a committed external interface." The real divergence is depth. **OpenTofu 1.10+ traces the graph walk**: `Validation`/`Plan`/`Apply phase` spans, then one span per graph node (`Plan resource instance changes`, `Apply resource instance changes`, `Configure provider`) carrying `opentofu.resource_instance.address` and `opentofu.provider.source`, which is what makes "which resource is slow" answerable. **Terraform 1.15 has ~10 span sites**, all in `main`, `checkpoint.go`, and `internal/command` — module and provider install, backend init — and **no graph-walk spans at all**; the instrumented plan/apply code lives in `internal/stacks/stackruntime` and `internal/rpcapi`, which HCP Terraform drives over RPC, not the CLI. Three consequences worth carrying: OpenTofu reads **`TRACEPARENT`** (1.10) so a run nests inside a pipeline trace while Terraform ignores it ([#35444](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35444), open since 2024-07-10); Terraform exports spans through a **`SimpleSpanProcessor`** (synchronous, per span) where OpenTofu batches; and **Terraform's root span is named `terraform <full command line>`**, so `-var="password=…"` reaches the tracing backend, where OpenTofu's root span is just `tofu`. Version-gated with `git tag --contains`: init spans, node spans and `TRACEPARENT` all first ship in **v1.10.0**, `OTEL_SERVICE_NAME` in **v1.11.0**, and 1.13 (unreleased) adds `TRACEPARENT` propagation into `local-exec` children plus OTel library logs in `TF_LOG`. Two CI traps recorded in **A3**: a broken collector **fails the command** (both binaries exit 1 while the variable is set), and `autoexport`'s default protocol is `http/protobuf`/**4318**, so the OpenTofu docs' own `4317` example needs `OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_PROTOCOL=grpc` — the docs page contains both port numbers in different sections. **A4** gains the one layer that emits **metrics**: HCP Terraform agents (`-otlp-address`, `tfc-agent.*` metric families, per-run attributes like `workspace_name`/`run_id`), which neither CLI does. Two stale claims fixed in [[feature-history]] and [[opentofu-feature-history]] ("no OTel in Terraform" / "None"). Notes: [[ot-otel-tracing]], [[otel-tracing-facts]]. **E5 and E3 stay ⬜** — no chapter was written, so nothing flips.

> **2026-08-13** — **[[code-styling]] captured (Anton Babenko's *Terraform Best Practices*), a new source folder, and it closes a real gap rather than restating one.** The coverage check was unambiguous: **`pre-commit`, `terraform-docs` and `.editorconfig` appear nowhere in the path** — absent, not name-dropped. The official [[tf-style-guide]] (cited in **B4**) gives the *rules*; this page gives the **toolchain that enforces them**, and the two barely overlap. Folded into three topics. **B4** gains a callout on style being tool-enforced rather than discipline-enforced: the `terraform_fmt` pre-commit hook, **`terraform fmt -check`** as a CI gate (exit 0 when clean, non-zero listing offenders — the difference between a gate and a no-op), and **`.editorconfig`** as the layer `fmt` cannot reach, since `fmt` never touches non-`.tf` files and a repo with a `Makefile` still needs tabs enforced. **A3** step 2 now specifies `-check` over bare `fmt` and pairs it with the local hook so the pipeline stops being where formatting is discovered. **I5** gains `terraform-docs` as the way a module README's input/output tables stay current, plus the registry trap that *"all links in `README.md` should be absolute"* — the Registry renders the README off-repo, so a relative link that works on GitHub resolves to nothing there. **One divergence recorded rather than adopted silently:** both sources prefer `#` over `//` and block comments, but the `# -----` / `######` section-header convention is the community book's own, absent from HashiCorp's guide and unenforced by any tool. ⚠️ Source caveats now on the course index: it is **not** a HashiCorp publication, its pages date themselves only as *"Last updated N ago"* (this one reads "1 year ago", so its pinned `rev: v1.99.4` is roughly mid-2025 and should be re-checked), and the page carries a visible `@todo` for module versions, releases and GH Actions — so that half is unwritten, not deliberately omitted. Nav mirrored at **rung 3** from the GitBook DOM; the book's structure is flat, ten top-level pages, so no groups were invented. No topic page yet — style/formatting now has two sources ([[tf-style-guide]] + this), so it goes to the topics backlog as promotable. Statuses unchanged: **B4** stays ✅, **A3** and **I5** stay ⬜.

> **2026-08-13** — **Last unverified claim in the four reference pages settled: `-target` and `-exclude` really are mutually exclusive in OpenTofu, and the rule is wider than stated.** It had been carried from the docs, never checked. Source confirms it (`internal/command/arguments/extended.go`), and adds a detail no summary carried: in **1.9** the guard covered only the two direct flags, erroring with *"-target and -exclude flags cannot be used together"*; **1.10 widened it to the two families** on the same commit that introduced `-target-file`/`-exclude-file` (`556ba25638`, `git tag --contains` → first in v1.10.0), so any target-side option combined with any exclude-side option now fails with *"The target and exclude planning options are mutually-exclusive. Each plan must use either only the target options or only the exclude options."* The operational consequence worth carrying into **I7**: a plan is either allow-listed or deny-listed, never both, so there is no way to say "everything except X, but only within module Y" in one run. Both OpenTofu pages updated; the docs-sourced wording "mutually exclusive with it" is replaced by the behaviour and its version boundary.

> **2026-08-13** — **Same completion pass on the [OpenTofu Release Feature Map](reference/opentofu-release-feature-map.md).** No pre-1.6 hole to fill there, since 1.6.0 *is* the first release, so the work was the schema sweep, extended to `internal/encryption` because the flagship feature registers its key providers and methods in code rather than declaring them in a schema. **The find that matters for A6/I6: the `unencrypted` encryption method has been registered and user-reachable since 1.7.0 and appears in no changelog.** It is the escape hatch for migrating state back *out* of encryption and the thing you pair with a fallback block during rollout, so a page that teaches state encryption without it teaches a one-way door. The **`external` *method*** (distinct from the external *key provider*, which is what 1.10's changelog actually announces) is likewise unannounced. **Two near-misses worth recording as method**: `static` and `xor` sit under `internal/encryption/keyprovider/` from 1.7 and 1.9 but are **not registered in `default_registry.go`**, so they are compliance-test fixtures, not features — checking the registry rather than the directory listing is what kept two fake features out of the page. Same for `dual_custody_test.go`. **A correction to yesterday's Terraform page:** I wrote that OpenTofu 1.12's `language` block is "a working mechanism" against Terraform's inert `language = TF2021`. Half right. Its **`edition` argument is reserved and inert in exactly the same way** (only `tofu2024` accepted, which is the default); the working part is the nested **`compatible_with "<software>"`** block. OpenTofu also still parses Terraform's `terraform { language = … }` and **ignores it outright**, with the source giving the reason: it cannot predict how a future Terraform edition would use it. Both pages fixed. **Two structural divergences now recorded**: OpenTofu has **no `state_store` equivalent** anywhere in `internal/configs`, so Terraform's pluggable-state-storage work has no counterpart there; and OpenTofu's config parser has **zero** `allowExperiments` gating on `main`, so unlike Terraform it never lands a block's schema a release early behind a flag — a block in an OpenTofu release is usable in that release. Also logged: the sweep reports `dynamic` vanishing at 1.7, which is a **file-move artifact** (`util.go` and `module_merge_body.go` were deleted), not a removal. No topic status changes.

> **2026-08-13** — **[Release Feature Map](reference/release-feature-map.md) completed: pre-0.5 history recovered, and a schema sweep for what the changelog never says.** Two holes the page itself had flagged, both now closed. **First, 0.1.0 through 0.4.2 exist after all** — the `v0.11` branch changelog stops at 0.5.0, but `git show v0.4.2:CHANGELOG.md` still carries the opening history, and it is worth having: **0.2.0 is where HCL replaced the original C configuration library**; **0.3.0 introduced modules, `terraform init`, `terraform destroy`, JSON state (previously binary), `create_before_destroy`, interpolated `count` with `count.index`, and `path.*`**; 0.3.5 added remote state over HTTP/Consul/Atlas; 0.3.7 made interpolations nest; **0.4.0 added `terraform_remote_state`, `terraform taint`, `self.*` in provisioners, and continuous state saving during apply**. So several things the path treats as "always been there" have dates now, and `taint` (0.4.0) turns out to predate its replacement `-replace` (0.15.2) by six years. **Second, a method that finds unannounced features**: dump every `Name:`/`Type:` schema literal in `internal/configs/*.go` at each release tag and diff consecutive versions. It confirms the changelog on `removed` (1.7), `ephemeral` (1.10), `identity`/`parallel` (1.12) and the whole test vocabulary (1.6–1.7), and turns up four things no changelog mentions. **`language = TF2021` has been a reserved-but-inert argument in the `terraform` block since 0.15**, a placeholder for a never-shipped "language editions" mechanism, still inert in 1.16-rc1 — and worth flagging because **OpenTofu 1.12's `language` *block* is a different construct that actually works**, so the same word means two things across the tools. **`ignore_nested_deprecations` (1.15)** is undocumented in the changelog though [[tf-block-module]] already found it. **`state_store`**, a `terraform`-block child for pluggable state storage, has sat behind `AllowExperimentalFeatures` since 1.13 and still is at 1.16-rc1, never appearing in any changelog including the EXPERIMENTS sections — the likely successor to the fixed built-in backend list, worth watching for **I6**. And the sweep exposed a dating trap: **`action`, `action_trigger`, the `.tfquery.hcl` parser, the `list` block, and `terraform query` all exist in 1.13.0**, alpha-gated, and became stable in 1.14. The changelog is right about *availability* while the source shows *arrival*, so a claim like "actions landed in 1.14" needs to say which it means. New Part 3 on the page records all of it.

> **2026-08-13** — **The same treatment for OpenTofu** ([OpenTofu Release Feature Map](reference/opentofu-release-feature-map.md)), built from all seven version branches (`v1.6`…`v1.12`) plus `main` for the unreleased **1.13**. Companion to the Terraform map below, and it starts at 1.6 because **OpenTofu 1.6 inherits Terraform 1.5.x wholesale** — the pre-fork history is the other page's job. Threads traced: **state encryption** (the one capability with no Terraform equivalent, extended every single release from 1.7 through 1.13), targeting (`-exclude` 1.9 → `-target-file`/`-exclude-file` 1.10), testing, backends, installation, language, CLI ergonomics. **Two patch releases changed behaviour and are invisible from the `x.y.0` sections**, which is the finding worth carrying: **1.11.4** made `enabled` invalid inside modules that contain local provider configurations, and the changelog *admits* it is a breaking change shipped in a patch; **1.11.2 reverted** 1.11.0's suggestion to use `-exclude` on provider-reported unknown-value failures, because providers report those spuriously. Also: **`issensitive` changed semantics in 1.11** (no longer returns a known result for an unknown value), which can newly fail at plan time for modules feeding it into `count`/`for_each` — relevant to A6 and B7. And **1.10's `pg` backend must not share a database with an older OpenTofu's**, since the locking implementation changed and mixing can lose data — relevant to I6. Confirms rather than corrects the existing OpenTofu attributions in [[feature-history]] and [[opentofu-feature-history]]: state encryption 1.7, early evaluation 1.8, provider `for_each` and `-exclude` 1.9, OCI and OTel and short-circuit operators 1.10, `enabled` 1.11, dynamic `prevent_destroy` and `destroy = false` and `-json-into` 1.12 all check out against the changelogs. No topic status changes.

Built from `git show origin/v<X.Y>:CHANGELOG.md` across all 21 version branches (`v0.11`…`v1.15`, plus `v1.16` at rc1), not from release blogs, so it is exhaustive where [[feature-history]] is selective. Two halves: **feature threads** tracing one capability across releases (testing 0.15→1.16, import 0.7→1.16, secrets 0.14→1.16, state locking 0.9→1.12, provider distribution 0.10→1.16, language, functions, refactoring, plan/apply UX, HCP/Stacks), and a **per-release catalogue** from 0.5.0 to 1.16.0-rc1. **The finding that matters for citation accuracy: features routinely land in patch releases, and the path has been dating several of them to the wrong minor.** `for_each` on resources is **0.12.6**, not 0.12.0; `try`/`can` are **0.12.20**; `terraform login`/`logout` are **0.12.21**; network mirrors are **0.13.2**; `output -raw` is **0.14.3**; `-replace` is **0.15.2** and `-refresh-only` plus drift reporting are **0.15.4** (the path already had these two right). Also recorded, and absent from [[feature-history]]: **0.14 removed the separate refresh phase** (refresh folded into planning on demand), **1.6 introduced partial knowledge about unknown values** (possible collection length, possible nullness), **1.8 added `show -json` `applyable`/`complete` flags**, and **1.3 began evaluating pre/postconditions on no-op instances during apply** so failures surface in the run that caused them. > **2026-07-17** — **Reference shapes are structural, and typed module outputs change what callers receive.** A contradiction inside Ch 7 (§2 "every resource reference is an object" vs §3 "references look like objects but aren't") turned out to be an instance-vs-container confusion: an *instance* is an object, a bare *type name* is not a value, and the container depends on the meta-argument. Chasing it found the shape line itself wrong — `count` gives a **tuple**, `for_each` an **object**, not the list/map the docs say (the docs [declare that conflation](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/language/expressions/types) openly; §2 is the section that breaks it). **Why structural:** a collection needs one element type and instances aren't guaranteed to share one — `for_each = { a = "str", b = 5 }` yields `a` with `input: string` and `b` with `input: number`, two different object types no map could hold. Resources take that path unconditionally (`evaluate.go:931/952`). **The find worth keeping:** modules *do* switch. `GetModule` branches on "does the module fully define all output types", so adding `type = string` to a module's output flips `module.c` from `tuple` → **`list`** and `module.f` from `object` → **`map`** (verified, TF 1.15.8). So output `type` (1.15+) does change the type callers receive — **but measuring that turned out to deflate it**: auto-conversion means callers can't tell, since a tuple splats, converts, and satisfies a `list(object(...))` constraint exactly as a list does. Ch 6's modest "improves matching across validation, plan, and apply" was right all along, and the B6 🔄 I raised against it was **my** over-claim, not the chapter's under-claim — retracted, along with a flatly false line in I5 saying callers "feel the difference". Noted in **B6**/**I5**/**E3** as a curiosity, not a reason. Finding a real mechanism is not the same as finding it matters. Also fixed: the splat section justified itself with "a `count` resource is a list" (it's a tuple — right conclusion, wrong reason). Two method failures recorded: `terraform_data` is the only provider-free resource *and* is atypical (dynamic attributes), and the `noDynamicTypes` branch that nearly reverted a correct fix lives in `GetModule`, not the resource path. Evidence: [[conditional-branch-evaluation]].

> **2026-08-11** — **TUR Ch 1 captured** ([[01-why-terraform]]), the third source on the [IaC fundamentals](topics/iac-fundamentals.md) topic page and the most opinionated. **What it adds to B1:** the **five-category taxonomy** (ad hoc scripts → configuration management → server templating → orchestration → provisioning), which reframes "Ansible vs Terraform" as a cross-category comparison rather than a head-to-head; the **10 → 15 servers** worked example, which *demonstrates* declarativeness where every other source asserts it (edit Ansible's `count` and you get 25 servers; edit Terraform's and you get `Plan: 5 to add, 0 to change, 0 to destroy`); and the two consequences that follow — procedural code doesn't capture state, and procedural code resists reuse. Also the seven benefits of IaC including *happiness*, the bus-factor argument, and the honest sidebar that **cross-cloud portability is a red herring** (Terraform gives you one language and toolchain, not one configuration). **Ch 1 of the book already answers B1's milestone**, so no 🔄 — this is enrichment, and the taxonomy plus the 10→15 example are the obvious candidates if that chapter is ever expanded. ⚠️ **The chapter is the most perishable in TUR**, being a June-2022 landscape survey, so the note carries a long *Version reckoning*, all of it verified 2026-08-11 into [[iac-tool-comparison]]: Terraform's **BSL relicensing (2023-08-10, all HashiCorp products incl. Packer)** makes Table 1-4's "Source: Open" row wrong and needs an OpenTofu column; **Terraform and Ansible now share an owner** (IBM closed HashiCorp 2025-02-27 and has owned Red Hat since 2019), so "independent commercial backing" no longer separates them; **Puppet repeated the pattern** — Perforce restricted packages Nov 2024, Vox Pupuli forked **OpenVox** 2025-01-21; **rkt** was archived by CNCF (2019) and **Apache Mesos** retired to the Attic (2025), so two named runtimes in the chapter are dead; **Terraform Cloud is HCP Terraform** since 2024-04-22; and Pulumi's paid-only state locking is gone (*"a basic file-based locking system is enabled by default for all DIY backends"*), though the non-transactional-checkpointing limitation stands. B1 stays ✅.

Brikman, *Terraform: Up & Running* 3rd ed., O'Reilly, first release 2022-09-19. Referenced in I4 and I5 since the path was written; now actually captured. **The [Modules](topics/modules.md) topic page gains its second book-length source** and a comparison table, since TID Ch3 and TUR Ch4 split cleanly: **read TUR for why each feature exists, TID for what the feature can do.** TUR arrives at inputs, locals and outputs because its running example broke without them; TID enumerates the type system, `validation`, `sensitive` and registry publishing, none of which TUR covers — it never mentions a module registry at all. **Three formulations worth keeping from TUR:** *"the input variables are the API of the module"*; **a variable is permission to change a value** (the port/protocol constants became `locals` precisely so callers could not override them accidentally — the author-side twin of [[tut-module-use]]'s NAT-gateway advice); and **prefer separate resources to inline blocks in a module**, argued from encapsulation rather than style, because a separate resource *"can be added anywhere"* while an inline block can only be added by the module that owns the resource. It is also the only source here with the **promotion workflow** end to end. **One correction filed against the book:** its *"Git tags are as stable as a commit"* is right against accidental drift and wrong against an adversary — a tag is a movable pointer, which is the supply-chain exposure I4 already records. ⚠️ **And a version finding big enough for the resources table:** TUR's running example is built on **`aws_launch_configuration`**, and AWS closed that path — no instance type released after 2023-01-01 works in one, and **accounts created on or after 2024-10-01 cannot create one by any method** ([[launch-configurations-eol]], new research-cache entry). Not a style nit; the example is unrunnable for a reader with a recent account, though none of the teaching depends on it. Its `aws_security_group_rule` advice also now points at a superseded resource. I4 and I5 both stay ⬜.

> **2026-08-08** — **The `module` block reference captured, closing the Modules docs section 8/8** ([[tf-block-module]]), inserted as I4 reference #5. It has absorbed the old *Module Sources* page, so it is the exhaustive `source` catalogue. **Things that live only here:** `//` marks a sub-directory within a package with query parameters placed *after* it, and since Terraform extracts the **whole** package to disk, sibling modules inside it can reference each other by local path — which is what makes A8's module-split shim resolve; **same `source`, different labels** is legal, and is the multi-region workaround forced by I8's static provider association; **absolute local paths are copied into the module cache** rather than referenced in place; **`localterraform.com`** resolves against whichever HCP/TFE instance runs the config; **HCP Terraform can authenticate to Git sources only with SSH keys**, repeated four times and prominent nowhere; and **`ignore_nested_deprecations`** (1.15) is the consumer's opt-out from an author's `deprecated` warnings. ⚠️ **The page contradicts itself on `source` ten lines apart** — *"You must specify a literal string… does not support template sequences or arbitrary expressions"* against *"The `source` attribute can reference constant input variables and local values"*. The second is current (1.15 dynamic sources); the first is stale text that survived a restructure, and the surrounding typo density fits a recently-rewritten page. Its `depth` *"default is 1"* also cannot be right — that would make every Git source shallow and break the SHA refs the same page documents, while the vendored `go-getter` takes its full-clone branch when `depth < 1`.

> **Correction to the 2026-08-08 `depth` entry below:** that entry called the `depth`-rejects-SHA restriction undocumented. **It is documented** — on this page, under GitHub, Git and BitBucket alike: *"you must specify a named branch or tag known to the remote repository. You cannot use raw commit IDs."* The two how-to pages I had read describe `depth` with no caveat, which is what I generalized from. The finding itself stands, and so does the I4 callout: **no page connects the restriction to the SHA-pinning advice**, so the cost still only surfaces when the pin fails. Corrected in [[tf-modules-configuration]], the I4 Git-sources callout, and `cache/search/git-module-shallow-clone-vs-sha-pin.md`.

> **2026-08-08** — **A8's primary reference captured** ([[tf-modules-refactoring]], HCDocs "Refactor modules"), folded into A8 reference #2 — the deep `moved` reference whose removal rules [[tut-move-config]] already quoted, with everything else new. **The governing rule is about addressing:** a bare address moves a resource *as a whole* and covers every instance, but an instance key on **either** side flips the block into instance mode. That single toggle is what makes `count` ↔ `for_each` ↔ neither migrations expressible, one `moved` block per index — and **neither [[tut-count]] nor [[tut-for-each]] mentions `moved` at all**, so the migration those tutorials walk through is a destroy-and-recreate as written. **One case is already free:** adding `count` to a resource that lacked it auto-proposes a move to index `0`, while `for_each` needs a key you pick and a rename is never free — which is exactly the "8 added, 4 destroyed" [[tut-count]] measured. **Two hard limits worth memorizing:** cross-*type* moves exist but are **provider-dependent** (ask the provider docs, not the language docs), and a managed resource can **never** become a `data` block — that path is `removed` with `lifecycle { destroy = false }` plus a separate `data` block. **The find worth keeping is the module-split shim**, which solves something nothing else in the docs addresses: splitting a *published* module without breaking consumers. The original module is kept as a shim that calls the new modules and carries the `moved` blocks, so existing users upgrade in place. HashiCorp flags it as violating the closed-box rule and attaches the precondition — *"all three of these modules are maintained by the same people and distributed together in a single module package"* — so this licence does **not** extend to writing cross-module `moved` blocks against someone else's module. Scoping bounds it: addresses resolve relative to the module they are written in, and *"a module may only make `moved` statements about its own objects and objects of its child modules"* — down, never up or sideways. Finally, **chaining** (`a→b`, `b→c`) is the mechanism that makes retention make sense: each block covers consumers arriving from a different starting version, so a module's `moved` blocks accumulate like database migrations rather than as a courtesy changelog. A8 stays ⬜.

Thinnest page in the Modules section and a third of it is ecosystem prose. **The find is a navigation trap:** the Configuration Language sidebar's **Publish modules** entry contains **none** of the publishing requirements — no repository-naming rule, no semver-tag rule, no structure requirement. Those live under the separate **Registry Publishing** top-level section, already captured in `cache/search/module-repo-naming-convention.md`, which now carries that pointer. A reader following this section's sidebar will not find them. Two things it does supply. **The cleanest one-line case for a registry source over a Git one** — *"The alternative sources do not support the first-class versioning mechanism"* — which is I4's registry-versus-Git argument compressed to a sentence. And **the public registry's curation model, stated plainly**: partner and community modules "both have their place… accessible to practitioners who can decide which modules best fit their requirements", i.e. **it hosts, it does not vet**, which is why [[tut-private-registry-add]]'s review checklist exists. ⚠️ Its own example omits the `version` argument on the very page selling version constraints. Also worth keeping: it recommends the standard module structure even for Git-distributed modules so they can *"be published on the registry at a later time"* — structure as a migration path, not just tidiness. I5 stays ⬜.
