> **2026-08-08** — **I5 gains its design reference** ([[tf-modules-composition]], HCDocs "Best practices for composing modules"), inserted as I5 reference #3 with the rest renumbered. The most substantial page in the Modules section, and **stricter than the enterprise guide it overlaps**: keep the tree **flat, one level of child modules**, wire them with expressions, because *"instead of a module embedding its dependencies… the module receives its dependencies from the root module, which can therefore connect the same modules in different ways to produce different results."* **The find worth keeping is that its headline pattern runs on the mechanism Ch 12 recorded as a footgun.** Its conditional-creation advice — never write a module that detects whether something exists; declare an `object(...)` input naming only the attributes you need and let the caller pass a `resource` **or** a `data` source — works because *"Terraform will allow any object that has at least these attributes"*, which is the same discard the Type Constraints page specifies and Ch 12 measured turning a caller's typo into a silent no-op. **So structural typing and typo detection are one knob turned two ways**: a *deliberately* partial object type is this pattern, an *accidentally* partial one is the footgun, and you cannot have both. OpenTofu's `Warning: Object attribute is ignored` makes it noisier for the pattern and safer against the typo. **Two more keepers.** *Assumptions versus guarantees* — an assumption is what must hold for a resource to be usable, a guarantee is what the rest of the configuration may rely on, and a `precondition` on an **output** is how a module asserts the latter (**A2**); an object type enforces shape while only a `validation` enforces content. And *data-only modules*, whose real trick is the last line of the page: **give a `join-network` module the same outputs as a `create-network` module and the two become swappable**, so promoting an environment from sharing infrastructure to owning it is a one-line `source` change — the most useful idea here for **A7**, and neither [[tut-organize-configuration]] nor [[tut-pattern-module-creation]] mentions it. Also a flat statement worth quoting back at people: Terraform *"intentionally does not attempt to abstract over similar services offered by different vendors"* because unification means a lowest common denominator; multi-cloud abstractions are yours to build from shared object types and matching output names. ⚠️ Two of its examples would fail `validate` — `aws_ami` takes **`owners`** (a list), not `owner`, and **`aws_subnet_ids` no longer exists** (docs 404 in the provider repo on `main`; `aws_subnets` returns 200) — plus its `recordsets` type requires `ttl` while the `for`-generated records omit it, which wants `optional(number)`. I5 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **I8 gains the module-boundary reference, and a rule cited five times finally gets its reason** ([[tf-modules-providers]], HCDocs "Providers Within Modules"), inserted as I8 reference #3 with the rest renumbered; it is the page [[tf-meta-providers]] explicitly defers all module-author guidance to, and that forward reference is now resolved. **The find that changes the story:** every tutorial in the Modules collection explains "no `provider` blocks in a module" by the `count`/`for_each`/`depends_on` conflict. That is the **later, mechanical** enforcement, added in v0.13. The actual reason is a lifecycle one — a provider configuration is required to *destroy* a resource as well as create it, and Terraform stores in state which configuration last applied each resource, so *"a provider configuration must always stay present in the overall Terraform configuration for longer than all of the resources it manages."* A module holding both would remove them simultaneously, leaving state pointing at a configuration that no longer exists, and planning errors until it is reintroduced. That generalizes past modules: **never delete a `provider` block while anything in state still references it.** **The inheritance asymmetry is the other keeper:** default configurations inherit, **aliased ones never do**, and **source/version requirements never do either** — *"especially important for non-HashiCorp providers"*, since a child omitting `required_providers` loses the source address and Terraform assumes `hashicorp/<name>`. **And it states plainly what [[tut-for-each]] could only infer:** the resource-to-provider association is *static*, so a `count`/`for_each` module call **cannot** vary providers per instance — multi-region fan-out is one `module` block per provider set, by design rather than omission. Also **closes an open docs discrepancy**: this page carries the corrected per-provider scoping (*"overrides the default inheritance behavior for that provider"*) while [[tf-meta-providers]] still carries the un-narrowed "cancels the default behavior" wording that issue [#35781](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35781) was filed against — the two reference pages disagree as they stand, and this one is right. Noted in both notes. Resolved in passing: [[tut-no-code-provisioning]] is not an exception to the rule, because a no-code module *is* the root module of the workspace HCP generates. I8 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **The standard module structure captured** ([[tf-modules-structure]]), folded into I5 reference #2 beside [[tf-modules-develop]]. The layout is a **machine-readable** convention, not a style preference — *"Terraform tooling is built to understand the standard module structure and use that structure to generate documentation, index modules for the module registry"* — which is why the registry's publishing bar requires it. **Four rules that cannot be guessed from the file tree.** A submodule's **`README.md` is its public/private marker**: *"Any nested module with a `README.md` is considered usable by an external user. If a README doesn't exist, it is considered for internal use only"*, and this is *"purely advisory"* — so a private submodule stays private by staying undocumented, which is an odd incentive worth knowing about. **The two source-address rules point opposite ways**: a root calling its own nested module uses a **relative** path so Terraform treats it as the same package rather than downloading it again, while a block in `examples/` must use **the external address**, because examples are expected to be copied into other repositories. **Resources may be split across files; `module` blocks may not** — nested calls stay in `main.tf`. And the root module *"is expected to be opinionated"* with nested modules as the escape hatch, which is the structural form of the 80%-of-use-cases rule. **One real conflict recorded:** this page says *"The README doesn't need to document inputs or outputs … because tooling will automatically generate this"*, where [[tut-pattern-module-creation]] instructs you to document required inputs, optional inputs and outputs in the README. Reconciled — the registry generates those tables from the `description` arguments, so writing descriptions *is* documenting the interface and a duplicated table only goes stale; what the guide adds on top is advertising default values and never defaulting a variable whose right value differs on every use. Smaller: use **commit-specific absolute URLs** in a module README or the registry renders them against the wrong version (the mutable-reference problem of I4's Git `ref` one layer up), and the page's own `minimal-module/` tree omits the `LICENSE` its prose says to always include. I5 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **I5's reference index captured** ([[tf-modules-develop]], HCDocs "Creating Modules" — the *Develop modules* overview), folded into I5 reference #2 and nested under the docs course's `Modules › Develop modules` group. Short page, kept for one section: **when *not* to write a module.** *"Over-using modules can make your overall Terraform configuration harder to understand and maintain"*; a good module *"raise[s] the level of abstraction by describing a new concept in your architecture"*; and the test worth memorizing — *"If you have trouble finding a name for your module that isn't the same as the main resource type inside it, that may be a sign that your module is not creating any new abstraction."* **Paired with [[tut-pattern-module-creation]]'s complexity test, the target gets sharp: a module should be nameable in a phrase that is neither a paragraph nor a resource type.** The two heuristics guard opposite failure modes, and together they qualify the tutorial's "aim for small and simple" — small is a scoping instruction, not a licence to wrap one resource. Noted honestly against it: `terraform-aws-modules/ec2-instance/aws`, which [[tut-module-use]] consumes, **fails the naming test but not the substance test** (at v6.4.0 it also creates an IAM instance profile, security group, EIP and EBS volumes), so the heuristic is a smell detector rather than a rule. Two smaller keeps: this is now the **third** official definition of "module" — container of resources *used together*, raising abstraction — and it is the only one that gives grounds to call a module a *bad* module, where [[tut-module]]'s is the mechanism and [[tf-modules]]'s is the unit. And its description of refactoring blocks, *"reinterpret existing objects as if they had been created at the corresponding new addresses"*, is the most precise one-liner on `moved` anywhere in the docs — and explains why [[tut-move-config]]'s plan still reported `3 to change`, since reinterpreting where an object came from says nothing about whether its desired configuration still matches. I5 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **I4's primary reference captured** ([[tf-modules-configuration]], HCDocs "Use modules in your configuration"), inserted as I4 reference #4 and filed under the docs course's new `Modules` group. It is the consumer-side reference behind [[tut-module-use]], and it covers five things that tutorial never mentions: Git `ref` selection, shallow clones, `const`-gated source expressions, `-replace` addressing inside modules, and removing a module without destroying it. **The find worth keeping is an undocumented incompatibility, and it sharpens I4's own Git-sources callout:** `?depth=1` — the documented way to avoid cloning a large repository's whole history — **rejects a SHA `ref` outright**. `go-getter` passes `--depth` together with `--branch <ref>` and `git clone --branch` accepts only a branch or tag, so a commit-shaped ref is caught by a regex and fails with *"note that setting 'depth' requires 'ref' to be a branch or tag name"*. Version-gated against `go-getter` **v1.8.6**, the release Terraform **1.15.8** vendors per `go.mod` in the local checkout at the release commit. So **immutable pin or shallow clone, not both** — a third cost of SHA-pinning alongside the two update-bot gaps already recorded, and neither docs page connects them. Marked as read from vendored source rather than reproduced. **Two smaller things:** the `const` requirement is a **timing** argument, not a safety one (modules install at `init`, before plan-time evaluation exists), and it constrains the whole dependency chain rather than the syntax — a `local` composed from const variables is legal in the source position; and **`init` honours what is already installed while `-upgrade` re-resolves the constraint**, which with no module lock file is precisely where an untested version lands. ⚠️ The page's removal section is garbled four ways, including a closing link to the **`moved`** block reference at the end of a section about **`removed`**. Also worth noting against the tutorials: its example pins the VPC module at **6.0.1**, a current major, where every page in the Modules tutorial collection still pins 3.x — the reference docs are maintained, the tutorials are not. Evidence: `cache/search/git-module-shallow-clone-vs-sha-pin.md`. I4 stays ⬜.

> **2026-08-08** — **The language reference's Modules section index captured** ([[tf-modules]]), inserted as I4 reference #3 ahead of Module Sources and creating a new `Modules` nav group under Configuration Language in the docs course (sidebar rung 1, positioned after Outputs as the source puts it). A thin index page, kept for two things. **It defines a module differently from the tutorials, and both definitions are load-bearing:** *"a collection of resources that Terraform manages together"* here, against [[tut-module]]'s *"a set of Terraform configuration files in a single directory"*. The structural one makes "every configuration is already a module" true; the semantic one is what makes the scoping question in I5 answerable — if a module is just a directory, "what belongs in it?" has no answer. **And it supplies the Develop → Distribute → Provision frame** the whole docs section is built on, which maps cleanly onto the tutorial collection just captured: Develop is [[tut-module-create]] / [[tut-module-object-attributes]] / [[tut-pattern-module-creation]], Distribute is [[tut-module-private-registry-share]] / [[tut-private-registry-add]], Provision is [[tut-module-use]] — and [[tut-move-config]] belongs to none of them, because refactoring is what happens after all three. Recorded for later capture: the section has **eight** pages and this is one, with `Refactor modules` and `Publish modules` already quoted secondhand (in [[tut-move-config]] and `cache/search/module-repo-naming-convention.md`) but not yet captured directly. Also noted: the page says "Every Terraform workspace includes configuration files in its root directory" where it means *working directory* — a loose use of a word that already means two other things ([[tf-state-workspaces]]). I4 stays ⬜.

> **2026-08-08** — **E6 gains its named hands-on, and the Modules collection is closed at 10/10** ([[tut-no-code-provisioning]], HCTut "Create and use no-code modules"). E6 reference #3 was already pointing at this tutorial by an alias URL with no notes behind it; it now has both, plus a worked-example callout in **A6**. **The design rule that defines no-code:** a workspace's entire change surface is *(variable values) × (module version)* — *"You cannot directly apply configuration changes to existing no-code workspaces"* — so anything the author did not expose is fixed until they ship a release, which turns [[tut-pattern-module-creation]]'s minimize-inputs advice into a hard requirement. **The find worth keeping is that it inverts the no-provider-blocks rule and, in doing so, explains why the rule ever existed:** provider blocks are forbidden in modules because they conflict with `count`/`for_each`/`depends_on` (the constraint [[tut-for-each]] measured), and *"Since users will not reference no-code modules in written configuration, there is no risk of this conflict."* The prohibition was never about providers. Its cost is that HCP spawns a workspace per provision, so credentials must arrive automatically — a project-scoped variable set in the tutorial, dynamic credentials in the page's own next steps. Governance lives in **Configure Settings**: pinning which version no-code deploys (pushing a tag publishes but does **not** roll out), and converting a free-text variable into a **dropdown of allowed values**, which is input validation at the UI layer rather than in HCL. **Also folded into A6:** its RDS module is the shortest complete `ephemeral` + write-only example available — one ephemeral password feeding `password_wo` and `value_wo`, with **both** `_version` companions pointed at a single local so the database and its SSM copy rotate together. ⚠️ Its own 1.0.1 upgrade adds a variable with **no default**, forcing every consuming workspace to answer a prompt — the breaking way to make an additive change, and a live counterexample to [[tut-module-object-attributes]], made worse by the fact that no-code users are by definition the people who don't write Terraform. Two things not to copy from the module: `publicly_accessible = true` on an RDS instance in a module meant to codify standards, and `cidr_blocks = ["192.80.0.0/16"]` described as an internal VPN when that is **public** address space (RFC 1918 is `10/8`, `172.16/12`, `192.168/16`). **One counter-observation:** this is the **only maintained page in the collection** — ephemeral resources (1.10), write-only arguments (1.11), `postgres16` — where the other nine carry 2022-era pins. E6 and A6 both stay ⬜.

> **2026-08-08** — **A8 gains its hands-on, and the Modules collection is now fully captured** ([[tut-move-config]], HCTut "Use configuration to move resources"), inserted as A8 reference #1 ahead of the reading, with a companion callout in **I7**. Ninth and last page of the collection, and the one that makes the other eight survivable on live infrastructure: extract a root resource into a local module *and* swap a hand-written module for a registry one, then reconcile with `moved`. **The find worth keeping is that the fixed plan is not empty** — `0 to add, **3 to change**, 0 to destroy. `moved` preserves **identity, not configuration**, so swapping implementations is a move *and* a config change landing in one apply (the registry module's defaults differ), while extracting your own code is a clean move that prints `has moved to` with no action symbol at all. **A zero-destroy plan is not a zero-impact plan** — read the `~` lines. Also recorded: the un-`moved` plan's tell-tale reason line *"because X is not in configuration"*, which is what an address in state with no matching block looks like whether you deleted it or renamed it; module-level `moved` cascading to every address beneath it but **requiring `terraform init`** because the install key changes; and `moved` blocks living in the **root** while naming in-module addresses on both sides, with `[0]` index keys taken from the destination module's internals. **Corrects the tutorial against its own reference docs on two points:** removing a `moved` block is safe *"when you are maintaining private modules within an organization and you are certain that all users have successfully run `terraform apply`"*, not never — the absolute phrasing is the page's, and the real rule is about whose state still holds the old address; and `moved` needs **v1.1**, not the v1.3 the page sets as a prerequisite. This is also the block [[tut-count]] needed and never named. A8 and I7 both stay ⬜.

> **2026-08-08** — **I5 gains its design theory, and the A4/A5 seam gets closed** ([[tut-pattern-module-creation]], HCTut "Module creation — recommended pattern"), inserted as I5 reference #5 with a new callout in **A5**. No code — it is an architecture guide from the *Recommended Enterprise Patterns* series, and it answers the question the rest of the collection skips: not which arguments should be variables, but **which resources belong in the same module at all**. **The three scoping axes are the most portable idea in the collection:** *Encapsulation* (group what always deploys together), *Privileges* (never span a segregation-of-duties boundary), *Volatility* (keep long-lived infrastructure out of a module that ships daily). The last two are blast-radius arguments, which is why they decide **state** boundaries as well as module boundaries — the same argument A7 makes one granularity up, and the principled version of what [[tut-organize-configuration]] demonstrates by destroying production from a dev file. **Also worth keeping:** its too-big heuristic (*"If a module's function or purpose is hard to explain, the module is probably too complex"*); the deliberate **minimize inputs, maximize outputs** asymmetry, since every input is a promise to maintain while an output is a value you already computed; the default test (`var.vpc_id` should never have one — *if the right value differs on every use, a default is a trap*); and the nesting trade, where an external child module can change behavior *"with no changes to the parent's calling code or version"* — an unpinned transitive dependency, I4's no-module-lock hazard one level deeper — while an embedded submodule is version-locked but unreusable. Its "avoid conditional expressions in an MVP" is **sequencing advice, not a contradiction** of [[tut-module-object-attributes]]: ship narrow, then add flexibility with `optional()` so the first consumer doesn't break. **The find that closes an open thread:** [[tut-private-registry-add]] left "approved module list" ambiguous, since curation enforces nothing; this page states the control outright — *"With Sentinel … only modules from the private Terraform registry can be used"* — so A4 publishes the set and **A5** makes it binding, now written into A5 as a worked-policy suggestion. ⚠️ Its naming table gives `terraform-azure-database`, but the provider is **`azurerm`** (verified against the Registry API; no `hashicorp/azure` exists), and the same three-row table mixes an underscore with hyphens. Third page in this collection to fumble that convention. Diagram captured. I5 and A5 both stay ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-08** — **A7 gains its hands-on** ([[tut-organize-configuration]], HCTut "Refactor monolithic Terraform configuration"), inserted as A7 reference #3. Seventh page of the Modules collection and it contains no modules — it makes the case *for* them by showing what a flat configuration costs. **The find worth keeping is its middle step, which demonstrates blast radius instead of describing it:** a shared `random_pet` feeds both bucket names, so changing its `length` in `dev.tf` — a file containing nothing but development resources — destroys and recreates production. So **splitting `main.tf` into `dev.tf` and `prod.tf` buys readability and nothing else**, because the coupling lives in the reference graph, not the filesystem. That is the sharpest available answer to "we separated our environments into files". Three more: **`-target` is named and dismissed** as "risky and not a sustainable way to manage distinct environments" (so it stays an I7 recovery tool, not a layout strategy); **converting one state into per-directory states needs `state mv` work**, which the exercise dodges by destroying everything first; and the local backend relocates non-`default` workspace state to `terraform.tfstate.d/<name>/terraform.tfstate`. ⚠️ **The page teaches CLI workspaces as environment separation, which A7 reference #2 exists to contradict** — the language reference marks *"Workspaces are not appropriate for system decomposition or deployments requiring separate credentials and access controls"* as **Important** ([[tf-state-workspaces]]), and dev/prod is the canonical case of the second clause. Its own mitigation is "be aware of the workspace you are working in", which is human vigilance standing in for an access boundary. Read its decision rule as being about configuration duplication, not isolation, and A7's existing position stands unchanged. Page defects: resource counts wrong twice over (says 5, its own prose describes 9, the repo defines 15), and it instructs you to comment out a `terraform` block the current repo does not contain — that repo has no `required_providers` anywhere, so nothing is version-pinned in a tutorial about growing a project safely. A7 stays ⬜.

> **2026-08-08** — **A4 gains the registry-curation hands-on** ([[tut-private-registry-add]], HCTut "Add public providers and modules to your private registry"), inserted as A4 reference #4. A five-minute UI page with no configuration in it, but it forced a boundary worth drawing between **A4 and A5**. The tutorial says curation lets you *"define a list of approved components"*, which reads like a control; the docs say the registry *"stores a pointer"*, that the feature *"designate[s] which public providers and modules are recommended"*, and that after removal *"Users in the organization can still use the removed provider or module without changing their configurations."* **Two things follow that the page never states:** curation does not change the source address (which is exactly why removal breaks nothing), and it enforces nothing at all — so "approved" is a label, and the actual control is Sentinel/OPA policy in **A5**, or the exact-pin discipline already noted in I4. Four omissions from the page also folded in: the permission is **Manage Private Registry**, not org ownership, so curation is delegable to a platform team; it is drivable through the Registry Providers/Modules **APIs**, which makes the approved list a reviewable artifact instead of hand-maintained drift; **Terraform Enterprise must reach the Algolia search host as well as `registry.terraform.io`**, or the search box silently returns nothing on an egress-filtered install; and **no-code provisioning can be enabled on a curated *public* module** (Standard/Premium), which is the E6 self-service story without authoring or maintaining a module — the tutorial's own Tip points only at building your own. Evidence appended to `cache/search/hcp-private-registry-publishing.md`. A4 stays ⬜.

> **2026-08-08** — **A4 gains the private-registry hands-on** ([[tut-module-private-registry-share]], HCTut "Share modules in the private registry"), inserted as A4 reference #3, with a companion callout in **I5** since publishing is the step after authoring. First page in the Modules collection with **no Community Edition path** — it is HCP-only and almost entirely UI. Facts worth holding: the private-module address is `<HOSTNAME>/<ORGANIZATION>/<MODULE_NAME>/<PROVIDER_NAME>`; **versions come from git release tags** (semver `x.y.z`, optional `v`, non-matching tags ignored, ≥1 tag required to publish), so versioning is a release-process decision in the repo rather than an HCL argument; and registry scope differs by product — HCP shares **within** an organization, Terraform Enterprise **within or across**. **The find worth keeping is the publishing-workflow split**, which the tutorial renders as a variant toggle and never explains: tag-based auto-imports on tag push, while branch-based needs a **Publish New Version** click and a commit SHA, cannot re-use a version number, and is **the only workflow supporting module testing** — which makes it the one to pick once `terraform test` (A2) is part of shipping a module. Branch-based versions still pin to a commit, so this is *not* the mutable-`ref` hazard from I4's Git-sources callout. ⚠️ Two errors on the page, both self-contradicting: it states the address format with a spurious literal `terraform/` segment, and it tells you to set the registry **Module Name** to the *repository* name (`terraform-aws-s3-webapp`), either of which would produce an address its own module block disagrees with. It does, however, state the `terraform-<PROVIDER>-<NAME>` convention **correctly as a repository rule** — so the collection contradicts itself, and [[tut-module]] is the page that has it wrong. **Both variants were then read in full, which turned up a content asymmetry rather than another typo:** the branch path removes the tag/release section outright, so a reader who picks that variant never meets the semver-tag rules at all, and the variant's own summary still claims they "created and versioned a GitHub repository" when the version was typed into HCP and the repo carries no tag. Read the tag variant first whichever workflow you intend to use. Evidence: `cache/search/hcp-private-registry-publishing.md`. A4 and I5 both stay ⬜.

> **2026-08-08** — **Ch 13 written, I4 → ✅.** Blends [[tf-modules]], [[tf-modules-configuration]], [[tf-block-module]], [[tf-expr-version-constraints]], [[tut-module]], [[tut-module-use]], [[tut-private-registry-add]] and TUR Ch 4 [[04-reusable-modules]]. **The chapter's spine is that `source` decides everything else** — whether `version` is legal, what pins the code, where it lands on disk, and whether an update bot can move it. **The headline measurement:** the same `main.tf` with `version = "~> 4.1"` ran `4.1.0` in one directory and `4.11.0` in a fresh one on the same machine, same day, no warning — because `.terraform.lock.hcl` records providers only and `modules.json` is never committed. Unpinned, the same module resolved **5.15.4**, a whole major away. **Second measured find:** the `init` / `init -upgrade` asymmetry holds for Git refs as well as registry versions, which makes the mutable-tag exposure demonstrable — `git tag -f -a v0.0.1 HEAD` was invisible to `plan`, then flipped the installed module on the next `init -upgrade` with the config byte-identical. **Third:** the `depth`+SHA restriction, which [[tf-block-module]] had only inferred from vendored `go-getter` source, is now **reproduced live** — `?ref=<sha>&depth=1` fails with `fatal: Remote branch <sha> not found in upstream origin (note that setting 'depth' requires 'ref' to be a branch or tag name)`, while the same address with a tag installs fine. **Fourth, and it corrects a note:** [[tut-module-use]] says local modules are **symlinked** into `.terraform/modules/`; on 1.15.8 that directory holds **only `modules.json`**, whose `Dir` points straight back at the relative source path. No copy, no symlink. The consequence the tutorial draws is still exactly right — a local-module edit is live with no re-`init`, and **both callers move together** (`0 to add, 2 to change`), which is the coupling versioned sources exist to break. **1.15 dynamic module sources measured end to end:** a `source` composed from two `const = true` variables through a `local` installs at `init`, accepts `-var` and `*.auto.tfvars`, and without `const` fails with `Only literal values and const variables can be evaluated during init.` **OpenTofu 1.12.4 diverges twice:** it installs the same variable-composed source **with or without** `const` (early evaluation since 1.8), and has **no `modules` command** at all (`OpenTofu has no command named "modules"`). Also captured: a child module contributes provider constraints to your resolution (`>= 5.83.0, ~> 6.0` from one `module` block), and `terraform get` installs modules without touching providers or the backend. Lab committed at `labs/chapter13/` (three parts, lab 3 self-contained via a local `git init` repo built by `setup.ps1`/`setup.sh`). ⚠️ Emulator fidelity note: Floci does not persist bucket tags, so a tagged bucket replans the same tag change forever — an emulator gap, not Terraform. Next: **I5 — Authoring modules**.

> **2026-08-08** — **I5 gains its interface-design hands-on** ([[tut-module-object-attributes]], HCTut "Customize modules with object attributes"), inserted as I5 reference #4; its CORS half also folded into **I3** reference #2, which had been an unsourced "build a security-group module" exercise. Nothing here contradicts Ch 12, so **I3 stays ✅** — the tutorial illustrates the chapter rather than revising it. The page's own argument for objects is API evolution: optional attributes *"make it easier for you to ship new module versions without changing the variables that module users need to define"*, which makes them the additive counterpart to the 1.15 `deprecated` mechanism already noted in I5. **Two transferable finds.** A shared prefix or suffix across flat variable names is **an object hiding in the interface** — `terraform_managed_files` becomes `files.terraform_managed`, the suffix having existed only because a flat namespace had nowhere to put the qualifier. And the module's type constraint should **mirror the wrapped resource's own required/optional split** (its `cors_rules` deliberately matches `aws_s3_bucket_cors_configuration`), or the module rejects valid configurations and defers errors to apply. **Ch 12's silent-discard footgun has no better illustration than this page, and the page never mentions it:** misspell `www_path` inside the `files` object and the object constraint drops the key, `optional(string)` fills `null`, the ternary falls back to the module's own `www` directory, and you ship a placeholder page with `validate` reporting `Success!`. ⚠️ Page defects beyond stale pins: its transcripts predate its own repo (`Plan: 6 to add` where a current clone gives 8, since the repo has since gained the ownership-controls and public-access-block resources), the "Clean up your infrastructure" section prints an apply that **adds** a resource under a `terraform destroy` heading, and the delete-these-four-variables snippet lists five. Its repo also omits the `depends_on` that `learn-terraform-modules-create` uses to order the bucket policy after the public-access block, leaving no graph edge between them — flagged as a graph reading, not a measured failure. One counter-observation worth keeping: **`hashicorp/dir/template` 1.0.2 is still the latest published version** (2020-07-29), so not every old pin is a stale pin. I5 stays ⬜.

> **2026-08-08** — **I5 gains its first hands-on** ([[tut-module-create]], HCTut "Build and use a local module"), inserted as I5 reference #3. The authoring counterpart to I4's two consumption tutorials, and its real content is interface design, not syntax: **a value your module's purpose fixes is not a variable** (its example is the ACL, which must be `public-read` for website hosting — the author-side twin of [[tut-module-use]]'s NAT-gateway advice), and *"outputs are the only supported way for users to get information about resources configured by the module"*. Also confirms **no `provider` block in a module** — [[tut-for-each]] already showed that's a hard constraint once `count`/`for_each` is on the module block, not the recommendation this page calls it. ⚠️ **This one is not merely stale, it is broken.** Two AWS defaults changed underneath it: new buckets are Bucket-owner-enforced so ACLs are disabled (`400 AccessControlListNotSupported`), and Block Public Access is on, so `PutBucketPolicy` rejects a `Principal = "*"` policy. The page's four-resource module trips both. **Its example repo is already fixed** and the rendered page is not — the repo carries `aws_s3_bucket_ownership_controls`, `aws_s3_bucket_public_access_block` with all four settings `false`, and `depends_on` chains, for six resources. Third page-vs-repo divergence in this collection and the only one where following the page fails outright. **The find worth keeping beyond S3:** those `depends_on` blocks are the *legitimate* kind, and they're now the counterexample in I1's "prefer an attribute reference" callout — the ACL consumes no attribute from either resource yet must be applied after both, so ordering-without-data-flow is exactly what `depends_on` is for. Smaller: the module outputs `website_domain` (*"used to create Route 53 alias records"*) where `website_endpoint` is the browsable URL, so the tutorial's own destroy log prints a bare regional suffix with no bucket name in it — a live example of the output-choice judgment the page is teaching. Evidence: `cache/search/s3-acl-bpa-defaults.md`. I5 stays ⬜.

> **2026-08-08** — **I4 gains its hands-on** ([[tut-module-use]], HCTut "Use registry modules in configuration", Community Edition variant), rewriting I4 reference #2. Two registry modules produce **22 resources** — the encapsulation benefit and the review problem in the same number. **The constraint worth memorizing:** *"Terraform will not display module outputs by default"* — a child module's outputs are readable inside the configuration but invisible at the CLI until the root module re-exports them, so `module.MODULE_NAME.OUTPUT_NAME` is a config-level reference, not a CLI-level one. **The best line on the page is about variables, not modules:** you should *not* declare a variable for a value your organization has already decided (its example is `enable_nat_gateway`) — a variable is a permission to vary, and declaring one hands the decision to the caller. The tutorial then does exactly that. Also confirms `count` on a `module` block (the [[tut-for-each]] two-axis mechanism, seen here in its plain form) and that **local modules are symlinked** into `.terraform/modules/`, so edits land without re-`init` or `terraform get`. ⚠️ **Staleness checked rather than assumed, and the result cuts the other way from [[tut-count]]:** the pins are years old (provider `~> 4.49.0`, vpc **3.18.1** vs. current 6.6.1, ec2-instance **4.3.0** vs. current 6.4.0) but every argument and output the tutorial uses is still declared at vpc **v6.6.1** and ec2-instance **v6.4.0** — verified against the tagged source 2026-08-08. So this one is a version bump, not a rewrite; the three bumps are coupled, since ec2-instance 6.4.0 alone forces AWS provider `>= 6.37`. Its hardcoded `ami-0c5204531f799e0c6` is the part not to copy (`ami_ssm_parameter` exists at v6.4.0). Rendered page vs. example repo disagree on the instance `name`, and here the **page** is correct. Cache updated: `cache/search/aws-modules-current-versions.md` gains the ec2-instance row and the surviving-interface check. I4 stays ⬜.

> **2026-08-08** — **I4 gains its conceptual opener** ([[tut-module]], HCTut "Modules overview" — first page of the Modules tutorial collection, new `Modules` nav group under HashiCorp Terraform Tutorials). Inserted as I4 reference #1, renumbering the rest. It is a no-code page, so what it buys is vocabulary and motivation: **every configuration is already a module** (the directory you run commands in is the *root module*; one reached through a `module` block is a *child module*), Terraform reads exactly one directory unless a `module` block sends it elsewhere, and the strongest case for modules is **security, not tidiness** — object storage has too many options to get right repeatedly, so encode the correct settings once. **Found an error in the source page:** its best-practices list says *"Name your **provider** `terraform-<PROVIDER>-<NAME>`"*, but that three-part name is the **module repository** convention (`terraform-google-vault`, `terraform-aws-ec2-instance`); providers use `terraform-provider-<NAME>`. Verified against [Publishing Modules](https://developer.hashicorp.com/terraform/registry/modules/publish) and folded into **I5** reference #2 with the rest of the registry's publishing bar (public GitHub repo, standard module structure, ≥1 semver tag). Cached at `cache/search/module-repo-naming-convention.md`. Glossary gains *root module*, *child module*, *no-code ready module*. Topic status unchanged — I4 stays ⬜ until a chapter exists.

> **2026-08-05** — **Ch 12 written, I3 → ✅.** Blends the dynamic-blocks and Type Constraints references, TID Ch 3 §3.6 and Ch 4 §4.10, and the splat/conditional notes. **The chapter's spine is that a type constraint is a conversion step, not a gate** — it can convert a value, silently discard part of it, or reject it, and only rejection is loud. **The find worth keeping:** an `object(...)` constraint deletes undeclared attributes by design, so a caller's typo becomes a **no-op** with `Success!` from `terraform validate`. Measured at a module boundary on 1.15.8. **Refined during review:** the discard is always silent, but whether it *hurts* depends on `optional()` — misspell a **required** attribute and Terraform rejects the call by name, misspell an **`optional()`** one and its default fills the hole so the mistake ships. The footgun needs both halves of the chapter: the object constraint removes the attribute, `optional()` removes the evidence. **OpenTofu 1.12.4 catches it and Terraform never does** (`Warning: Object attribute is ignored`), but the boundary is narrower than it first appeared — OpenTofu warns for module calls and variable defaults and stays **silent for `.tfvars`**, which is where much real input arrives. **Second measured find:** `optional()` and a variable `default` disagree about `null`. A variable default covers absence only and keeps an explicit `null`; an `optional()` default covers both, always, behaving like `nullable = false` with no opt-out — which is exactly why the docs can promise `optional()` is never `null` in the receiving module. **Third:** `dynamic "lifecycle"` and `dynamic "nonexistent"` give the *same* error, so the meta-argument rule is invisible to anyone who doesn't already know it. Also verified against the AWS provider at tag **v6.54.0**: the canonical `dynamic "ingress"` security-group example is one the provider now advises against, recommending `aws_vpc_security_group_ingress_rule` with `for_each` instead — resource iteration (I1) displacing block iteration for that resource type, though `dynamic` remains the only answer wherever a provider hasn't promoted its nested blocks. **A fourth find came out of the lab, once the provider would load:** generated blocks do not keep declaration order, and the two ways they lose it are different. Through a provider **set** (`aws_security_group.ingress`) rules declared 443, 80, 5432 come back 80, 443, 5432 — the provider's order, not yours, and not predictable from the input. Through a **map** (`for_each` over lifecycle rules) they come back in **sorted key order**, deterministically, every run — `logs, tmp, everything` declared renders `everything, logs, tmp`. So a map-driven dynamic block is the one whose output order you can state in advance, which is Ch 10's `for_each`-over-`count` argument one level down. Also measured: `optional()` defaults are visible in the plan (rules omitting `cidr_blocks` got the default, all three omitting `protocol` got `"tcp"`), and the zero-or-one toggle is an **in-place update** (`0 to add, 1 to change, 0 to destroy`) rather than a replacement. Lab committed at `labs/chapter12/` (four parts); all four ran, **and Parts A and B were then re-run under OpenTofu 1.12.4 with no divergence** — same blocks, same defaults, same set reordering, same sorted-key map order, so block generation is tool-independent and the reordering is the provider's doing rather than either CLI's. **That re-run also corrected a line in Ch 2:** `.terraform.lock.hcl` is keyed by the fully-qualified provider address, so Terraform's lock names `registry.terraform.io/hashicorp/aws` while OpenTofu wants `registry.opentofu.org/hashicorp/aws` from the identical `source` — running `tofu` against Terraform's lock fails with `Inconsistent dependency lock file` before it plans anything. Ch 2 had said the lock file "behave[s] the same in both tools"; the format and purpose do, a written lock file does not transfer, and the two registries even resolved `~> 6.0` to different patches on the same day (6.58.0 vs 6.57.1). Facts cache: `docs/research-cache/dynamic-blocks-facts.md`. Next: **I4 — Using modules**.

> **2026-08-03** — **Ch 11 written, I2 → ✅.** Blends the `lifecycle` reference, the State-collection tutorial, TID Ch2 §2.7.2 and Ch5 §5.7, the `resource`/`removed`/`data` block references, `terraform_data`, and the OpenTofu divergences. **The chapter's spine is the state fact:** lifecycle rules live only in the configuration, except `create_before_destroy`, so `prevent_destroy` dies with the block that carries it. Measured end to end in `labs/chapter11/lab1` — the destroy is rejected with `Error: Instance cannot be destroyed`, then deleting the block plans `1 to destroy` with no error and applies. **A documented claim was corrected by measurement:** the docs say a dependency that inherits `create_before_destroy` *cannot* override it to `false`. It can be written, and Terraform **silently discards it** — no error, no warning, only `ForcedCBDTransformer: forcing create_before_destroy on for …` in a trace. Identical under OpenTofu 1.12.4. The note, the topic page and this page's I2 callout were all corrected. **Also measured on 1.15.8:** propagation writing `create_before_destroy = true` into the state of a bucket that never declared it; `+/-` versus `-/+` and the `(deposed object 6fc41790)` line; a fixed-name DynamoDB table failing create-first with `ResourceInUseException` while leaving state clean; `ignore_changes` holding a drifted tag with `0 changed` while config and state disagree; `ignore_changes = all` valid but both `[all]` and `["all"]` errors; `replace_triggered_by` firing on an *in-place update* of its `terraform_data`, and rejecting a variable with *"Only resources, count.index, and each.key may be used"*; and dynamic `prevent_destroy` failing on Terraform with *"Variables not allowed"* while validating on OpenTofu. Lab committed at `labs/chapter11/` (five parts). Next: **I3 — Dynamic blocks & complex types**.

> **2026-08-03** — **I2 gains its hands-on** ([[tut-resource-lifecycle]]), inserted as I2 reference #2 — one exercise per rule on an EC2 instance plus security group, from the **State** tutorial collection (a collection this project had not touched before; new nav group). **What it adds over the reference page is all terminal output.** `prevent_destroy` fails at *plan* with no override flag, and Terraform's own suggested escape is `-target` — one of the few places HashiCorp recommends it. `create_before_destroy` flips the plan symbol from `-/+ destroy and then create replacement` to **`+/- create replacement and then destroy`**; read literally, the symbol is the operation order, and nothing else in the plan announces CBD is active (confirmed at tag **v1.15.8**, `internal/command/jsonformat/plan.go:688`). The apply log then shows the prior object being destroyed under a **deposed key** — current Terraform renders it `(deposed object 940b3833)` (`internal/command/views/hook_ui.go`, v1.15.8), where the tutorial shows an older bare-parenthesis form. **The unstated cost of CBD:** a deposed object left behind by a failed apply stays in state and is destroyed by the next plan. Neither the reference nor the tutorial says so. (Corrected 2026-08-03 while reviewing Ch 11: it does **not** show in `terraform state list`, which prints only current objects — use `terraform show -json` and read `deposed_key`.) For `ignore_changes` the tutorial manufactures real drift with `aws ec2 create-tags`, then shows apply reporting `0 added, 0 changed, 0 destroyed` while state refreshes to the **drifted** value — the rule suppresses the plan to update, not the read, so config and state legitimately disagree until a replacement. **It also accidentally demonstrates the propagation from the previous entry:** the instance depends on the security group, so CBD was enabled on the security group too, invisibly — invisible only because that group is updated in-place. Had the port change forced it to be replaced, create-first would have collided on the unique `name`. Written up as an I2 experiment. ⚠️ Tutorial targets Terraform 0.14 and AWS provider 3.26.0, and typos `drift_example` as `Drift_example` in one code block. I2 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-03** — **I2's primary reference captured** ([[tf-meta-lifecycle]]), inserted as I2 reference #1 and closing the last uncaptured meta-argument page. **The find that reframes the topic:** *"Except for `create_before_destroy`, Terraform does not explicitly record a resource's lifecycle rule to state."* That is the mechanism behind the already-noted `prevent_destroy` caveat — the guard lives in the config, so deleting the block deletes the guard — and it makes I7's `removed` block with `destroy = false` the only real protection. **The find that is new:** `create_before_destroy` **propagates along dependency edges and cannot be switched back off** — enable it on A and Terraform enables it on everything A depends on, writes it to state, and rejects an override to `false` because that would imply a graph cycle. Confirmed in source at tag **v1.15.8**: `ForcedCBDTransformer` (`internal/terraform/transform_destroy_cbd.go`) runs in the **plan** graph builder, and state v4 carries `create_before_destroy` per instance (`internal/states/statefile/version4.go:719`), with the field comment giving the reason — destroy ordering must still work after the config is gone. Since CBD is opt-in precisely because some object types cannot coexist with their replacement, propagation can push an upstream resource into a mode it cannot support, from an edit you made elsewhere. Also captured: `ignore_changes` is **honored on create and ignored only on update** (which is what makes the AMI case work), takes relative addresses with index notation (`tags["Name"]`), and cannot target a meta-argument; the three `replace_triggered_by` trigger conditions, where resource/instance references watch a *planned action* but an attribute reference watches a *value*; and the `precondition`/`postcondition` ordering guarantees — preconditions run before argument evaluation and after `count`/`for_each`, a failed postcondition blocks every downstream resource. Unusually for this docs set, the applicability data holds up: `lifecycle` has no **Supported constructs** matrix at all, and the per-rule lines scattered through the page agree with [[tf-block-data]]. I2 stays ⬜ — the note is a reference, not the chapter.
>
> **2026-08-02** — **I8's `providers` reference captured** ([[tf-meta-providers]]), inserted as I8 reference #2 and closing one of the three meta-arguments the 2026-08-01 entry listed as uncaptured. **The find is a reframing, not a fact:** the map is a **remap, not an injection**. Keys are configuration names used *inside the child*, values are configuration names *in the parent*, so `providers = { aws = aws.usw2 }` rewrites what the child's own `aws` means rather than handing it something new. The child keeps writing plain `aws_*` resources with no `provider` argument, and the caller decides which region they land in — which is why the argument exists: you retarget a module **without editing it**. Neither [[tf-meta-arguments]] nor [[tf-provider-block]] says this; both describe the argument as "passing configurations in". **Two applicability facts:** `providers` is optional *only* while the child declares no `configuration_aliases` (declaring one makes it mandatory, since non-default configurations are never inherited), and it applies to Stacks' **`component` and `removed`** blocks — the index page's table lists `module` alone, a fourth instance of the lower-bound rule. **The defect:** this page still says supplying `providers` "cancels the default behavior" so the child "only has access to the provider configurations you specify" — the exact un-narrowed wording [terraform#35781](https://github.com/hashicorp/terraform/issues/35781) was filed against. The correction landed on *Providers Within Modules* and never propagated here, so the docs now disagree with themselves; per-provider is the accurate reading, and the I8 warning box already said so. Folded into [[providers]] and [[meta-arguments-lifecycle]]. I8 stays ⬜; *Providers Within Modules* is still uncaptured and is where the module-author guidance lives.
>
> **2026-08-01** — **I1 gains the `for_each` hands-on lab** ([[tut-for-each]]), inserted as reference #5. **The thing worth the whole tutorial:** `count` and `for_each` are illegal in the same block, and this is the only HashiCorp page that shows the way around it — **move the `count` resource into a module, put `for_each` on the module block**, so the two multiply without sharing a block. Neither reference page mentions it. It comes with a constraint stated in a bare Note: a module using `count`/`for_each` **cannot declare a `provider` block** and must inherit from its caller, so the trick buys multiplication but not multi-provider fan-out — which is exactly the gap OpenTofu's core-language `provider for_each` fills and Terraform confines to Stacks. Both now on [[meta-arguments-lifecycle]], along with the chaining-vs-re-iterating distinction (this tutorial re-iterates `var.project` across five blocks and correlates with `[each.key]`, rather than chaining). **Two statements on the page are wrong, both re-verified locally on TF 1.15.8:** "the `for_each` argument also supports lists" — `for_each = ["a","b"]` fails with *"must be a map, or set of strings, and you have provided a value of type tuple"*; and "`each.key` is the index of the item in the collection" for sets — it is the **member string**, the instance address is `["alpha"]`, and `each.key == each.value`. That second sentence is the one a reader is most likely to internalize, since it is the only place either page explains sets in terms of `each.key`. **Also recorded:** its own Note warns against `for_each` for independently-lifecycled environments and its example then puts `dev` and `test` in one configuration, so one destroy takes both (75 destroyed); and it re-addresses everything again (74 added / 39 destroyed) without mentioning `moved` — the second consecutive tutorial to teach refactoring without state refactoring. One corroboration: this page uses the **correct** `security_group_id` for the same module at the same pin where [[tut-count]]'s page uses `this_security_group_id`, confirming that one is a stale rendering rather than a real API difference. I1 stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-08-01** — **I1's `for_each` reference captured** ([[tf-meta-for-each]]), completing the pair with [[tf-meta-count]]. Folded into I1 reference #3 and a new `for_each` section on [[meta-arguments-lifecycle]]. Most of the page is capability `count` has no equivalent for: **chaining** (`for_each = aws_vpc.example`, where `each.value` is a whole upstream object — the docs argue for it on *coupling*, since it "tells Terraform to expect the instance keys for both to always change together", not on brevity), the **sensitive-value ban** (an error, not a warning: keys are "always disclosed in UI output", so a secret can never be an instance key — escape is `toset([for k, v in local.map : k])`), and the **refusal to implicitly convert lists or tuples to sets**, which is why `toset()` is mandatory and is the one place the docs' type story is strict. **Existing correction reinforced:** the page's "accepts a map or a set of strings" is still incomplete — the implementation also accepts **objects**, so `for_each = { a = "1", b = "2" }` needs no `tomap()`, and the page's own chaining section calls a `for_each` resource "a map of objects" when it is an object. Already recorded from [[conditional-branch-evaluation]]; now attributed to the page that states it. **New find:** this page's **Supported constructs** list omits **`action` and `import`** — both described in its own examples — doubling the `count` page's single omission and reinforcing the lower-bound rule on the topic page. **Divergence sharpened:** the page lists `provider` under **Stack** configuration blocks, so Terraform *does* support provider iteration, just not in ordinary modules. [[ot-provider-for-each]] updated — the OpenTofu difference is the configuration surface, not the feature. Glossary gained `each.key`/`each.value` and `for_each` chaining. I1 stays ⬜; `lifecycle`, `provider`, and `providers` remain uncaptured.

> **2026-08-01** — **I1 gains the `count` hands-on lab** ([[tut-count]], HCTut "Manage similar resources with `count`" — the same tutorial [[tf-meta-arguments]] links to as `/tutorials/0-13/count`). Inserted as I1 reference #4. Contributes two patterns the reference page never shows: **the count is a product, not a literal** (`var.instances_per_subnet * length(module.vpc.private_subnets)`, so adding a subnet scales the fleet), and **modulo round-robins instances across buckets** (`count.index % length(...)`) — a clean illustration of where `for_each`'s criterion does *not* apply, since these distinct values **can** be derived from an integer index. Both now on [[meta-arguments-lifecycle]]. **The find:** the exercise is a refactor, and its own apply output reads **"8 added, 0 changed, 4 destroyed"** — renaming `aws_instance.app_a` → `aws_instance.app` rebuilt everything, because a resource address *is* its identity in state. `moved` blocks (TF 1.1+) are the fix and the tutorial requires 1.2+, so they were available; it never mentions them. Teaching a refactor without the refactor-safety tool leaves the reader thinking a rename normally costs a rebuild. **Two currency defects recorded:** the page's `elb_http` snippet references `this_security_group_id`, an output name **dropped in security-group module v4.0** while the page pins v4.9.0 — its example repo is correct, only the rendered snippet is stale, so the tutorial works if you clone and breaks if you copy-paste; and every pin is majors behind (AWS provider `~> 4.22` vs 6.x, vpc 3.14.2 vs 6.6.1, security-group 4.9.0 vs 6.0.0, elb 3.0.1 vs 4.0.2). Verified against the module's `outputs.tf` at v4.9.0, the example repo's `main.tf`, and the Registry API; cached at `cache/search/aws-modules-current-versions.md`. Also flagged: its `aws_instance.app.*.id` is the **legacy** splat ([[tf-expr-splat]]). I1 stays ⬜.

Closes a dangling gap: the page had been fetched on 2026-07-10 and quoted inside [[tf-meta-arguments]] for its two corrections, but never written up, so the addressing rules lived nowhere. Folded into I1 reference #3 and into a new `count` section on [[meta-arguments-lifecycle]]. **The find:** the page's **Supported constructs** list omits **`action`** — while the same page's opening paragraph *and* one of its own use cases both describe `count` in an `action` block. That makes three mutually inconsistent applicability lists across two pages (index vs `count` on `data`, index vs `depends_on` on five block types, `count` vs itself on `action`), so the working rule is now "**treat every applicability list in these docs as a lower bound**", and the page *body* beats the page's own summary list. Covers: why state exists (real-world linkage / reduced complexity / performance / state-only resources), the three storage properties (resiliency/security/availability), the **tfstate JSON** field-by-field (`version` vs `terraform_version`, `serial`, `lineage`, `resources`/`outputs`/`check_results`) with the **sensitive-in-plaintext** and **root-outputs-only** rules, backends (built-in, `local` = dev-only, partial config + `-backend-config`, `cloud` block, `-migrate-state`, CLI-vs-HCP workspace overloading), safe state manipulation (`moved` 1.1 / `removed` 1.7 first → `state rm`/`replace-provider` → hand-edit last), the **four drift categories** and their fixes, `terraform_remote_state` (root outputs, `defaults`, security-vs-data-source tradeoff), and the state-only providers (`random`/`time`/`null`/`terraform_data`). **Currency correction folded in:** the book's Table 6.1 "S3 requires DynamoDB for locking" is stale — S3 native locking (`use_lockfile`) landed in **TF 1.10 / OpenTofu 1.10**, DynamoDB deprecated (see [[version-facts]], [[feature-history]]); flagged in the chapter's version callout. > **2026-07-29** — **HCDocs "State" overview captured** ([[tf-state]]) — the reference cited by **B9** step 2 now has a note behind it. Three things the book treatment doesn't state as sharply: state's *primary* purpose is storing **bindings** between remote objects and resource instances (not the file's contents); the **one-to-one mapping rule** is an invariant Terraform only guarantees while it does the creating and destroying, so `terraform import` and `terraform state rm` hand the invariant back to the operator; and `terraform output -json` / `terraform show -json` are the **supported** parse targets for external software, since the state format itself is explicitly allowed to change between versions. Glossary gained *One-to-one mapping rule*. Nav gained a **State** group mirroring the docs sidebar, which also relocated the previously-flat `removed block reference` note into it. Topics backlog **state** now names four sources. Status unchanged — **B9** stays ⬜ until the chapter is written.
>
> **2026-07-29** — **HCDocs "Purpose of Terraform State" captured** ([[tf-state-purpose]]) — the why-state-exists argument behind **B9** step 1. One reason here is **absent from TID Ch6 §6.1**, which otherwise covers the same ground: state retains each resource's most recent **dependency set**, and it does so for **destroy ordering**. Delete a resource from the configuration and the edges that would order its destruction are deleted with it, so the order must come from state. The rejected alternative (a built-in per-resource-type ordering hierarchy) would need Terraform to know ordering semantics for every resource in every provider *and* across providers. Also new: the AWS-tag mapping was a shipped early prototype, not a hypothetical; the attribute cache is "the most optional feature" of state; and `-refresh=false` + `-target` make "the cached state the record of truth", which silently disables detection of the accidental-manual drift category from Ch6 §6.6. Glossary gained *Retained dependencies*. When **B9**'s chapter is written, the dependency-retention reason should join Ch6's four. Status unchanged — **B9** stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-07-29** — **HCDocs "State Storage and Locking" captured** ([[tf-state-backends]]) — the State-section counterpart to the Backends reference in **I6** step 2. A backend has exactly two responsibilities: store state, and provide a locking API, with **locking optional and not universally supported**. The operationally useful guarantee: a non-local backend "will not persist the state anywhere on disk" — **except** when writing to the backend fails, in which case Terraform drops state locally to avoid data loss and the operator must push it manually once fixed. That exception is worth knowing for **A6**, since it leaves a plaintext state file on a machine the guarantee otherwise excludes. Also records the `state push` guards (differing lineage, higher serial, both `-force`-able) and cross-references TID Ch6 §6.3.3, where the lineage check was traced in the 1.15.8 source and found **narrower** than this page's plain "Terraform will not allow this" — empty existing state is always overwritable, a missing lineage on either side is allowed, and the check never runs during a routine plan/apply. Doc-staleness flagged: the page still lists `terraform taint` among working commands, deprecated since **v0.15.2** in favour of `apply -replace`, with no deprecation notice in the 1.15.8 CLI help. The `state push`/`pull` half also serves **I7**. Status unchanged — **I6** and **I7** stay ⬜.

> **2026-07-29** — **HCDocs "Refactor Terraform state" captured** ([[tf-state-refactor]]) — the **cross-configuration** refactor guide, folded into **A8** step 1 alongside the module-refactoring reference it complements. `moved` blocks relocate addresses inside one state; this page is about moving resources *between* state files, which `moved` cannot do. The recommended path is now **`removed` + `import` blocks (1.7+)**, with `terraform state mv -state/-state-out` (1.0+) explicitly labelled "a legacy command" carrying "some risk of corrupting your remote state". Also captured: the four refactor triggers, the three grouping properties (volatility, stateful-vs-stateless, team ownership), the three dynamic-reference options ranked data source → `tfe_outputs` → `terraform_remote_state` (the same ranking [[tf-remote-state-data]] justifies on security grounds), and the six-step verification where **empty plans on both sides** are the proof. Two flaws in the page's own example are flagged in the note: it writes the state backup to `terraform.tfstate.backup`, the filename the local backend uses for its own automatic backup, and the apply transcript imports a different instance ID than the rest of the page. The `destroy = false` requirement on the `removed` block is cross-linked to [[tf-block-removed]], where it was verified on 1.15.6 — omit it in a refactor and the resource you are migrating gets destroyed. Status unchanged — **A8** stays ⬜.

> **2026-07-30** — **HCDocs "Remove a resource from state" captured** ([[tf-state-remove]]) — the forget-without-destroying how-to, folded into **I7** step 2 and its `removed` danger callout. Two constraints this page adds that no other note had. **`from` rejects instance keys** — `"aws_instance.example[1]"` is not accepted, so a `count`/`for_each` resource is forgotten in full or not at all. And **removal is not undone by editing configuration**: resuming management requires an **import**. Also the reason HashiCorp prefers the block over `terraform state rm`, stated explicitly for the first time in the docs we've captured: the `removed` block goes through plan, so the operation can be previewed; `state rm` mutates state immediately. Worth noting this is the one State page that gets the destroy semantics right on first reading — it never claims `removed` is inherently non-destructive, unlike the block reference itself ([[tf-block-removed]]). Status unchanged — **I7** stays ⬜.

> **2026-07-30** — **HCDocs "State Locking" captured** ([[tf-state-locking]]) — the page [[tf-state-backends]] defers to, cited by **I6** step 2 since the path was written and now backed by a note. Sharpens three things the path stated loosely. Locking covers **every operation that could write state**, not just `apply`. It is **silent on success** — "you do not see any message that it happens" — so no output is not evidence that no lock was taken. And acquisition failure is **fatal**: "if state locking fails, Terraform does not continue." The `force-unlock` callout was scoped tighter to match the docs: it is for **your own** lock after automatic unlocking failed, not for clearing a colleague's stuck run, and the lock ID is a **nonce** for one acquisition, so a stale ID will not release the current lock. The page never mentions `-lock-timeout`, so the note pairs it with the `terraform plan` command reference (verified 2026-07-30) — under contention the choice is three-way: fail now, retry for a duration, or skip the lock. Status unchanged — **I6** stays ⬜.

> **2026-07-30** — **HCDocs "Workspaces" captured** ([[tf-state-workspaces]]) — the CLI-workspaces page cited by **A7** step 2, and already a primary source behind the [[workspaces]] topic page; now traceable to a note of its own. The fact worth extracting is one neither the topic page nor TID Ch6 had: the docs **enumerate the backends that support named workspaces** — AzureRM, Consul, COS, GCS, Kubernetes, Local, OSS, Postgres, Remote, S3. **`http` and `oci` are absent**, so "remote backend" and "supports multiple states" are separate properties, and a backend choice does not settle the workspace question. Folded into A7 step 2 and into the topic page's state-layout section. Also noted: the page's *Current Workspace Interpolation* heading is pre-0.12 vocabulary — `terraform.workspace` is a **named value**, usable bare in any expression, and the page's own `count` example uses it that way. Status unchanged — **A7** stays ⬜.

> **2026-07-30** — **HCDocs "Backend block configuration overview" captured** ([[tf-backend-configure]]) — the first reference **I6** step 2 names, and the configuration counterpart to [[tf-state-backends]]. Three things folded into the path. The **three block limitations**, of which "a `backend` block cannot refer to named values" is the one that shapes real setups — it is *why* partial configuration exists, and **OpenTofu 1.8 lifts it** ([[ot-early-eval-backend]]), making this a live Terraform/OpenTofu divergence rather than a version lag. The **leak path stated at the source**: backend config is written in plaintext to `.terraform/terraform.tfstate` **and captured into every plan file**, which yields a consequence the path had not recorded — a saved plan applies with its **own frozen backend config**, so short-lived credentials inside it can expire before the apply. And the documented **`*.backendname.tfbackend`** filename convention for `-backend-config` files, which [[06-state-management]] §6.4 shows as `backend.tfvars`; both work, but `.tfvars` misfiles backend input as variable input. Nav gained a **Backends** group mirroring the docs sidebar. Status unchanged — **I6** stays ⬜.

> **2026-07-30** — **HCDocs "`local` backend" captured** ([[tf-backend-local]]) — first of the per-backend pages, folded into **I6** step 2. Confirms the default backend **locks** (through system APIs), which matters because [[tf-state-locking]] says locking is optional and backend-specific. Two arguments only: `path`, and **`workspace_dir`** — the knob behind the `terraform.tfstate.d` layout [[workspaces]] traces to the source. The section worth knowing is the legacy one: **`-state` / `-state-out` / `-backup`** apply to the local backend (and to *no* backend block at all), and setting them **overrides workspace-based state filename selection entirely** — with all three set, `terraform workspace select` no longer changes which file is read or written. Also two defaulting traps: `-state` without `-state-out` overwrites the input file, and `-state` without `-backup` turns the state filename into a backup *prefix* (`-backup=-` disables backups). HashiCorp recommends against all of it "even if you are running Terraform in automation". Status unchanged — **I6** stays ⬜.
>
> **2026-07-30** — **HCDocs "Remote State" captured** ([[tf-state-remote]]) — the conceptual page behind **I6** step 1, folded in there, with its one genuinely new idea folded into **A4**. Local state makes two separate things a human responsibility, **freshness** and **exclusion**; the remote store fixes the first and locking the second, which is why the page keeps them in separate sections. Its delegation framing is the useful one: remote state is a **read-only sharing channel between configurations** "without relying on any additional configuration store" — the same team-ownership split [[tf-state-refactor]] lists as a refactoring trigger, seen from the consuming side, and the reason [[tf-remote-state-data]]'s whole-snapshot warning matters. **New to the path: HCP's run queue is not a state lock.** A state lock covers one command and is released when it ends, so two people can each plan against the same state and the second apply invalidates the first's reviewed plan; HCP "detect[s] attempts to create a new plan when an existing plan is already awaiting approval, by queuing Terraform operations in a central location", holding the whole plan-then-approve cycle. S3 with `use_lockfile` gives the lock, not the queue. Also noted: this page names `consul_keys` as the Consul reader while [[tf-remote-state-data]]'s table names `consul_key_prefix` — the provider (hashicorp/consul v2.23.0) ships both as data sources, so the pages pick different halves. Status unchanged — **I6** and **A4** stay ⬜.
>
> **2026-07-30** — **HCDocs "`terraform refresh` command" captured** ([[tf-cmd-refresh]]) — folded into **I7**'s `-refresh-only` callout, which **overstated the command's standing**: it said "deprecated-in-spirit", but the CLI reference deprecates it outright in its second sentence. Corrected. The precise facts it adds over [[05-terraform-plan]]: the command is "effectively an alias for `terraform apply -refresh-only -auto-approve`", and `-auto-approve` "is always enabled" — there is no flag that makes it prompt. It also rejects a saved plan file and any planning mode but refresh-only. The documented hazard is **misconfigured** credentials (TID frames it as credentials *expiring* mid-run): a provider that cannot see its objects reports them gone, and refresh removes them from state "without any confirmation prompt" — nothing is destroyed in the cloud, but Terraform forgets it owns anything. Standing advice goes past "use the flag": "avoid using `terraform refresh` explicitly and instead rely on Terraform's behavior of automatically refreshing existing objects as part of creating a normal plan." [[feature-history]] gained the deprecation row (0.15.4). Nav: the CLI group now mirrors the CLI sidebar's own sections (**Inspecting Infrastructure**, **Manually Update State › Inspecting State**) instead of listing commands flat. Status unchanged — **I7** stays ⬜.

> **2026-07-30** — **HCDocs "Inspect Infrastructure Commands Overview" captured** ([[tf-cli-inspect]]) — the section index for `graph`, `output`, `show`, `state list`, `state show`, folded into **B3** step 4 beside [[tf-cli-commands]]. Mechanics are all elsewhere; the value is the framing. These are the sanctioned **integration surface** — "you can use these to integrate other tools with Terraform's infrastructure data" — which is the CLI-side statement of the rule [[tf-state]] gives from the state side: parse `terraform show -json` / `terraform output -json`, never the state file, because the format may change between versions. Two scoping details recorded: `output` reads **top-level** outputs only (the same root-module restriction that forces passthrough blocks in [[tf-remote-state-data]]), and both `state` subcommands are scoped to the current working directory **and workspace**, so an empty `state list` can mean the wrong workspace rather than nothing managed. Also noted while checking citations: `terraform state show` has no note yet, though [[tf-state-refactor]] step 2 depends on it for finding an import ID. Status unchanged — **B3** stays ✅ (this only adds a reference).

> **2026-08-09** — **Ch 7 §8 gains `flatten` and `setproduct`; a gap the path had been citing around.** Both functions were referenced as the answer to nested and combinatorial `for_each` inputs — in Ch 12 §5's shaping tip, in Ch 12 exercise 5, and in [[tf-expr-dynamic-blocks]] and [[tf-meta-for-each]], which both defer to the function guides rather than showing the pattern. Neither was ever taught: they appeared only as link chips in Ch 7's function-category table. Ch 7 §8 now works both through with `terraform console` output measured on **Terraform 1.15.8** — `flatten` descending all the way down rather than one level and tolerating unwrapped elements beside wrapped ones, the map-of-groups → one-element-per-pair → keyed-map pipeline, and `setproduct` returning **tuples indexed positionally** (`p[0]`/`p[1]`), with two arguments as the minimum. The tip added: which one you need is a question about the input, since `flatten` reads a pairing already present in the data and `setproduct` builds one that is not there yet. Ch 12 §5's tip now points at it instead of asserting it. Status unchanged — **B7** stays ✅, **I3** ✅.

> **2026-07-30** — **HCDocs "`terraform output` command" captured** ([[tf-cmd-output]]), and it settled a **contradiction between two HashiCorp pages**. The CLI reference says "Terraform does not redact sensitive values when you specify the output by name"; the language *Output Values* page says the opposite, showing `terraform output database_password` → `database_password = <sensitive>`. **Verified on v1.15.8: the CLI page is right.** `terraform output` (no argument) redacts; `terraform output password` prints `"notasecurepassword"` in the clear, as do `-raw` and `-json`. So `sensitive = true` protects the aggregate listing and nothing else. [[tf-outputs]] had faithfully copied the false claim from its source and is now corrected in place with the transcript; **[[tut-outputs]] and book Ch 6 already had it right**, so no chapter flips to 🔄. Second finding: the CLI page says ephemeral values are omitted "even if you specify an output by name", but on 1.15.8 that case is **unreachable** — an ephemeral output is rejected in a root module (`Ephemeral outputs are not allowed in context of a root module`) and a root output cannot derive from a child's ephemeral output (`Ephemeral value not allowed`), while `terraform output` reads root outputs only. Marked ❓ in the note rather than called a docs error, since the sentence may be forward-looking. Folded into **A6**'s redaction warning. Status unchanged — **B6** stays ✅, **A6** ⬜.
>
> **2026-07-30** — **HCDocs "`terraform state list` command" captured** ([[tf-cmd-state-list]]) — added to **B3** step 4 beside the inspection group. Two things the path never recorded about a command it uses constantly. The listing order is **defined**: "module depth order followed alphabetically", so your own root resources are always at the top and nested-module ones at the tail. And **`-id=` is a reverse lookup** — given a provider-assigned ID it prints the address that owns it, which is the answer to "the console shows me `sg-1234abcd`, which resource is that?" without grepping state. Verified on v1.15.8 against the emulator lab state, which also turned up an asymmetry worth knowing in scripts: **a missing `-id` is silent (no output, exit 0)** while a missing address filter is a hard `Error: Unknown resource` with exit 1 — so `-id` cannot be used as an existence check by exit status. Status unchanged — **B3** stays ✅.
>
> **2026-07-30** — **HCDocs "`terraform show` command" captured** ([[tf-cmd-show]]) — folded into **B3** step 4 and into **A3**'s plan-comparison callout. The `-json` output is **not one shape**: for a state file it is the state alone, for a plan file it is "the plan, configuration, and current state". Verified on v1.15.8 — state JSON has `['format_version', 'terraform_version', 'values']`, plan JSON adds `configuration`, `prior_state`, `planned_values`, `resource_changes`, `output_changes` — and it matches the plan file's physical layout (`tfplan`/`tfstate`/`tfstate-prev`/`tfconfig/`) verified earlier in [[tf-backend-configure]]. **New constraint for A3:** `show -json` has a schema-freshness precondition. After a provider upgrade the state must be brought forward first, and "if you are viewing a plan, it must be created **without `-refresh=false`**" — so the cheap-plan flag can cost you the JSON rendering the pipeline depends on. Also flagged: this page's remedy is "run `terraform refresh` first", a command its own CLI reference deprecates ([[tf-cmd-refresh]]); use `terraform apply -refresh-only`. Status unchanged — **B3** ✅, **A3** ⬜.
>
> **2026-07-30** — **HCDocs "`terraform state show` command" captured** ([[tf-cmd-state-show]]) — closes the *Inspecting Infrastructure* group, all five commands now noted, and fills the gap flagged when [[tf-cli-inspect]] landed (the command [[tf-state-refactor]] step 2 needs for an import ID). Two things. The page states outright that its output is "**intended for human consumption, not programmatic consumption**" and redirects to `terraform show -json` — the bluntest version of the split [[tf-cli-inspect]] frames. And its **PowerShell quoting advice is wrong on PowerShell 7**: it says to write `'…worker[\"example\"]'`, but verified on **pwsh 7.6.3 / TF 1.15.8** the backslashes pass through literally and Terraform answers `Error parsing instance address`. Single quotes are already literal in pwsh, so the address goes in exactly as `terraform state list` prints it: `'terraform_data.worker["example"]'`. The page's **cmd.exe** row is correct — confirmed through `cmd /c`. This is a second, distinct PowerShell trap from the `-flag=value` splitting already recorded. Status unchanged — **B3** stays ✅.

Captured the overview page ahead of writing Ch 8: the plan-vs-apply deferral rules (a `data` block whose args depend on a resource changing this plan reads at *apply*, showing `(known after apply)`; otherwise it reads during *refresh* before planning), `depends_on` timing (0.13+; on 0.12 it forced apply-time reads), custom `precondition`/`postcondition` in `lifecycle`, `count`/`for_each` on data blocks (`data.<NAME>[<KEY>]`), aliased-provider reads, and the local-only specialized sources (`template_file`, `local_file`, `iam_policy_document`). Also captured the **`data` block reference** ([[tf-block-data]]) — the built-in argument catalog: `count`/`for_each` (mutually exclusive; `for_each` takes a map or set of strings), `depends_on`, `provider`, and a `lifecycle` limited to `precondition`/`postcondition` (no destroy-side rules, literal values only).
